Was courage needed? They conquered by fighting. Was faith required? Trusting others to be like themselves, they made a treaty and let go those they could have killed. Was justice to be proved? Even the senate proved it by its silence, when those same Numantines, through their envoys, asked back either the peace alone, unbroken, or all those whom they had let go alive on receiving the pledge of peace. Did mercy seem to need examining? They gave enough proof of it, both by letting an enemy army go to life and by not receiving Mancinus to punishment. Was Mancinus, I ask, to be handed over, who held up the shield of a sworn treaty and drove off the slaughter hanging over a beaten army, who saved the endangered strength of his country for better times? Or, if the treaty that was agreed displeased you, why was the soldier ransomed by this pledge, or received when he returned, or not given back when he was asked for? Or, if whatever provision was made for saving the soldiers pleased you, why was Mancinus, who agreed it, alone handed over? Lately Varro, to enter battle too hastily, pushed on his colleague Paulus as he struggled against it, hurled the trembling army headlong, and at Cannae, notorious for Roman disasters, did not draw up his unhappy forces for battle but set them out for death; there he lost more than 40,000 Roman soldiers by his impatience alone, from which Hannibal had long assumed his victory. Having lost his colleague Paulus too, and what a man!, at last he dared most shamelessly to return to the city almost alone, and earned the reward of his shamelessness. For thanks were given him publicly in the senate because he had not despaired of the commonwealth, which he himself had brought to despair. But Mancinus, who laboured not to lose an army trapped by the fortune of war, was condemned by the same senate to surrender. I know, Romans, that in Varro's case too this displeased you, but it was yielded to the times; and in Mancinus's case this pleased you, but it was assumed according to the times; and so from the beginning you have brought it about that neither does a citizen take fitting counsel for the ungrateful nor does an enemy faithfully trust the faithless. Meanwhile Brutus in Further Spain crushed in a most bitter and difficult war 60,000 Callaeci who had come to help the Lusitanians, although he had surrounded them off their guard: of them 50,000 are reported killed in that battle and 6,000 taken; a few escaped by flight. In Nearer Spain the proconsul Lepidus stubbornly tried to conquer the Vaccaei, a harmless and suppliant people, even though the senate forbade it. But soon he suffered a very heavy disaster and paid the penalty for his wicked stubbornness. For 6,000 Romans were most justly cut down in this unjust war, and the rest escaped stripped of their camp and having lost even their weapons. This disaster under Lepidus was no less shameful than that under Mancinus. So let these times now be credited to them as happiness, I will not say by the Spaniards, beaten and worn out by so many wars, but at least by the Romans themselves, subdued by such continual disasters and so often overcome; so as not to reproach them with how many of their praetors, how many legates, how many consuls, how many legions and how great armies were consumed, I turn over only this: to what madness of fear the Roman soldier was dulled, so that he could no longer even hold his ground and steady his spirit for a trial of war, but, fleeing at once on seeing a Spaniard especially as enemy, he believed he was beaten almost before he was seen. By this argument it is plain that those times were judged wretched on both sides, since the Spaniards, even though they could conquer, unwillingly left their sweet leisure and bore foreign wars, and the Romans, the more shamelessly they thrust themselves on the quiet of others, were the more shamefully defeated.

## 5.6

In the consulship of Servius Fulvius Flaccus and Q. Calpurnius Piso a boy was born at Rome of a slave woman with four feet and four hands, four eyes and as many ears, and double in his male parts. In Sicily Mount Etna belched out and poured forth vast fires, which, rushing headlong down the slopes like torrents, burned up everything nearest with consuming flames, and scorched things farther off with hot embers flying widely about with heavy vapour: a kind of monster always native to Sicily, which is used not to foretell evil but to bring it. In the territory of Bononia crops grew on trees. So in Sicily a servile war arose, which was so heavy and fierce in the number of slaves, the equipment of their forces and the greatness of their strength that it terrified, I will not say the Roman praetors, whom it utterly routed, but the consuls too. For 70,000 slaves are said to have then conspired in arms, except for the city of Messana, which kept its slaves, treated generously, at peace. But Sicily was the more wretched in this too, because it is an island and never had a proper right over its own condition, subject now to tyrants, now to slaves, the former exacting slavery by wicked lordship, the latter exchanging it for liberty by perverse presumption, and above all because, shut in by sea on every side, it cannot easily drive an internal evil out. For it nourished a viper's brood to its own ruin, grown by its own lust and bound to live by its own death. And in this, the stirring up of a slave revolt is as much more savage as it is rarer than others, because a free multitude is moved with the aim of enlarging its country, a slave multitude with the aim of destroying it.

## 5.7

In the 620th year from the founding of the city, since the treaty agreed at Numantia was loading the Roman brow with almost greater infamy than once at the Caudine Forks, Scipio Africanus was made consul by the consent of all the tribes and sent with an army to storm Numantia. Numantia, a city of Nearer Spain, set not far from the Vaccaei and the Cantabri at the head of Gallaecia, was the farthest of the Celtiberians. For fourteen years, with only four thousand of its own, it not only withstood forty thousand Romans but even defeated them and bound them with shameful treaties. So Scipio Africanus, on entering Spain, did not throw himself at the enemy at once, as if to surround them off their guard, knowing that this kind of men is never so relaxed in body and mind into leisure that it does not surpass the preparations of others by the very quality of its condition; instead he exercised his soldiers in camp for some time as if in schools. And though he spent part of the summer and the whole winter without even trying a battle, even so he gained almost too little by this diligence. For when the chance of fighting came, the Roman army, overwhelmed by the charge of the Numantines, turned its back; but at the rebukes and threats of the consul, who threw himself in their way and held them back with his hand, at last, indignant, it returned against the enemy and forced those from whom it was fleeing to flee. It is hard then to win belief in the telling: the Romans both put the Numantines to flight and saw them fleeing. So although Scipio rejoiced and gloried because it had happened beyond hope, yet he declared that nothing further was to be dared in war against them. So Scipio judged that he must press on with the unexpected success: he shut the city itself in by siege, and also surrounded it with a ditch, ten feet wide and twenty deep. Then he strengthened the rampart itself, fronted with stakes, with frequent towers, so that, if any sally were attempted on it by an enemy breaking out, he would fight no longer as besieger against besieged, but, the other way round, as besieged against besieger. Numantia, set on a hill not far from the river Durius, embraced three miles in the circuit of its wall; although some assert that it was both small in site and without a wall: from which it is credible that, when they were pressed by war, they enclosed this space for the sake of feeding and guarding their cattle, or even for the convenience of working the land, while they themselves held a small citadel fortified by nature; otherwise so wide a space of city seemed rather to betray than to protect so few men. So the Numantines, long shut in and butchered by hunger, offered their surrender, if bearable terms were ordered, often also begging the chance of a fair fight, so that they might be allowed to die as men. At last they all broke out suddenly by two gates, having first drunk deeply, not of wine, which that place does not bear, but of a juice of wheat made by art, which juice they call caelia from heating. For the strength of the germ of the soaked grain is stirred up by fire, and then dried, and afterwards, ground into flour, is mixed with a soft juice, and by this fermenting a harsh flavour and the heat of drunkenness are added. So, warmed again by this drink after long hunger, they offered themselves to battle. The fight was long fierce and dangerous even for the Romans, and once again the Romans would have proved by fleeing that they were fighting against Numantines, had they not been fighting under Scipio. The Numantines, when the bravest of their men had been killed, gave way in the battle, yet in ordered ranks, and not as men fleeing did they return into the city; they refused to receive the bodies of the slain when they were offered for burial. With the last hope of despair, all determined on death, they themselves set fire to the closed city from within, and all alike were consumed by sword, poison and fire. The Romans had nothing at all from these conquered people except their own safety; for they did not reckon, when Numantia was overthrown, that they had conquered the Numantines so much as escaped them. The victor's chain held not one Numantine; Rome did not see what it gave a triumph for; gold or silver that might have survived the fire there was none among poor men; the fire consumed their arms and clothing.

## 5.8

At the time when these things were done at Numantia, the seditions of the Gracchi were being stirred up at Rome. Scipio, after Numantia was destroyed, while settling the other peoples of Spain in peace, asked a certain Thyresus, a Celtic chief, by what means the state of Numantia had either stood unconquered before or been overthrown afterwards. Thyresus answered: "Concord made it unconquered, discord was its ruin." The Romans took this as an example, as if it had been said to them and about them, since news was already being brought them of the seditions of the whole city in discord. With Carthage and Numantia destroyed, among the Romans useful consultation for the common good died, and infamous strife for ambition was born. Gracchus, tribune of the plebs, angry with the nobility because he had been marked among the authors of the Numantine treaty, laid down that land held until then by private persons should be divided among the people. When Octavius, tribune of the plebs, opposed him, he took away his office and gave him Minucius as successor. For these reasons anger seized the senate and arrogance the people. And at that time, as it happened, Attalus, son of Eumenes, dying, had by his will ordered the Roman people to succeed as heir to the rule of Asia. Gracchus, seeking the people's favour with a price, carried a law that the money that had been Attalus's should be distributed to the people. When Nasica opposed it, Pompeius too promised that he would prosecute Gracchus as soon as he had left office.

## 5.9

When Gracchus was striving to remain tribune of the plebs himself in the following year, and on the day of the elections was inflaming the people to sedition, the nobility, set on fire at Nasica's urging, routed the plebs with fragments of benches. Gracchus, fleeing by the steps that are above the Calpurnian arch, having thrown off his cloak, was struck by a fragment of a bench and fell, and rising again was killed by another blow, of a club driven into his brain. Two hundred more were killed in that sedition and their bodies thrown into the Tiber; the unburied corpse of Gracchus himself rotted too. Besides, the contagion of the servile war that had arisen in Sicily infected many provinces far and wide. For at Minturnae 450 slaves were crucified, and at Sinuessa up to 4,000 slaves were crushed by Q. Metellus and Cn. Servilius Caepio; in the mines of the Athenians too the same slave revolt was broken up by the praetor Heraclitus; at Delos also slaves swelling with a new rising were crushed when the townsmen forestalled them; all this apart from that first kindling of the Sicilian evil, from which these sparks, as it were, flew out and sowed these various fires. For in Sicily after the consul Fulvius the consul Piso stormed the town of Mamertium, where he killed 8,000 runaways, and those he could capture he nailed to the gibbet. When the consul Rutilius succeeded him, he too recovered by war Tauromenium and Henna, the strongest refuges of the runaways: more than 20,000 slaves are said to have been butchered then. Wretched indeed and inextricable is the cause of such a war. The masters would surely have perished had the sword not met the slaves as they grew insolent. But yet in those most unhappy losses of the fight and the still more unhappy gains of victory, as many as perished of the conquered, so many did the conquerors lose.

## 5.10

In the 622nd year from the founding of the city P. Licinius Crassus, consul and pontifex maximus, was sent with a very well-equipped army against Aristonicus, the brother of Attalus, who had seized Asia, handed over to the Romans by will; he was helped besides by great kings, that is Nicomedes of Bithynia, Mithridates of Pontus and Armenia, Ariarathes of Cappadocia and Pylaemenes of Paphlagonia, and by their very great forces; yet when battle was joined he was defeated; and when, his army driven to flight after very great slaughter, he himself was already surrounded by the enemy and almost captured, he drove the rod that he used for his horse into the eye of a Thracian; the barbarian, blazing with anger and pain, ran Crassus through the side with his sword. So by a way of death he thought out he escaped both disgrace and slavery. The consul Perperna, who had succeeded Crassus, on hearing of the death of Crassus and the disaster of the Roman army, flew in haste into Asia, attacked Aristonicus in an unexpected war as he was taking holiday after his recent victory, stripped him of all his forces and put him to flight; and when he had surrounded with a siege the city of Stratonicea, to which he had fled, he forced him, butchered by hunger, to surrender. The consul Perperna, seized by sickness at Pergamum, died; Aristonicus was strangled in prison at Rome by order of the senate. In the same year the wretched life of Ptolemy, king of the Alexandrians, produced a more wretched end of life. For he knew his own sister in incest and then took her in marriage, and at last cast her off more shamefully than he had married her. His stepdaughter, that is the daughter of his sister and wife, he took as wife, and killed his own son, whom he had had by his sister, and also his brother's son. For this, hateful for such incests and murders of kin, he was driven from his kingdom by the Alexandrians. At the same time Antiochus, not content with Babylon and Ecbatana and the whole rule of Media, joined battle with Phraates king of the Parthians and was defeated. Though he seemed to have a hundred thousand armed men in his army, he dragged along more than two hundred thousand camp servants and sutlers mixed with harlots and actors. So he was easily crushed by the strength of the Parthians and perished with his whole army. In the consulship of C. Sempronius Tuditanus and M. Acilius, P. Scipio Africanus, who the day before had declared before an assembly the danger to his life, because he had learned that it was being threatened by wicked and ungrateful men as he laboured for his country, was found dead in his bedroom the next morning; and I should not without reason count this among the greatest evils of the Romans, above all since the vigour and restraint of Africanus counted for so much in that city that it was readily believed that while he lived neither a social nor a civil war could arise. Some say he was killed by the treachery of his wife Sempronia, the sister of the Gracchi, so that the wicked family, born, as I believe, to the ruin of its own country, should be still more monstrous, amid the impious seditions of its men, by the crimes of its women too. In the consulship of M. Aemilius and L. Orestes, Etna, shaken by a vast tremor, overflowed with balls of fire, and again on another day the island of Lipara and the sea near it all round boiled so greatly that it dissolved even the scorched rocks, scorched the planks of ships when their wax had melted, boiled the dead fish floating on the surface, and suffocated people too, except those who could flee farther off, by the breathing in and out of hot air, which burned their vital parts within.

## 5.11

In the consulship of M. Plautius Hypsaeus and M. Fulvius Flaccus, a horrible and unheard-of destruction followed upon Africa as it was barely resting from the ruins of wars. For immense multitudes of locusts had gathered over the whole of Africa, and they had not only scraped away all hope of crops and eaten all the grasses with part of their roots and the leaves of the trees with the tender parts of the branches, but were even gnawing the bitter bark and the dry wood, when they were snatched up by a sudden wind, driven together into masses, carried long through the air, and plunged into the African sea. When the waves, as the water pressed on far out, had driven immense heaps of them over the widely stretched shores, the rotting and putrid mass gave off a smell far too foul and deadly beyond belief, from which there followed so great a pestilence of all living things alike that the putrid corpses of birds, cattle and beasts, destroyed by the corruption of the air, increased the plague of corruption everywhere. But how great the plague of human beings was, I myself shudder in my whole body as I tell it: for in Numidia, where Micipsa was then king, 800,000 people are recorded to have perished, and around the sea coast, which lies chiefly along the shore of Carthage and Utica, more than 200,000; and at the city of Utica itself 30,000 soldiers, who had been stationed for the defence of all Africa, were wiped out and swept away. This disaster pressed on so suddenly and so violently that at Utica at that time, in one day, more than 1,500 dead of those young men are said to have been carried out by one gate. Yet, by the peace and grace of almighty God I would say it, of whose mercy and in trust in whom I say these things: although in our times too locusts sometimes arise, and in different parts, and often do harm, but bearably, never in Christian times has so great a force of inextricable evil befallen that the plague of locusts, which could in no way be borne when alive, did more harm dead, and that, while it lived, everything was bound to perish, yet when it was destroyed it would have been more to be wished by all, as they perished, that it had not perished.

## 5.12

In the 627th year from the founding of the city, in the consulship of L. Caecilius Metellus and Q. Titius Flamininus, Carthage in Africa was ordered to be restored, and in the twenty-second year after it had been overthrown it was at last restored and filled, families of Roman citizens being brought out to live in it; a great prodigy came before. For when the surveyors sent to mark out the land of Carthage found that the stakes, the markers of the boundaries, that they had fixed had been torn up in the night by wolves and gnawed with their teeth, it was doubted for some time whether it was in the interest of the Roman peace for Carthage to be rebuilt. In the same year C. Gracchus, brother of that Gracchus who had already been killed in sedition, made tribune of the plebs amid uproar, was a great ruin to the commonwealth. For when he had often stirred up the Roman people to most bitter seditions with largesse and excessive promises, above all over the agrarian law, for which his brother Gracchus too had been killed, at last he left the tribunate to his successor Minucius. When Minucius, tribune of the plebs, had overturned the statutes of his predecessor Gracchus for the most part and repealed his laws, C. Gracchus with Fulvius Flaccus, surrounded by a huge column, went up to the Capitol, where an assembly was being held: there, when the greatest uproar had been raised, a certain herald was killed by the Gracchans, and it was like a signal for war. Flaccus, girt with his two armed sons, with Gracchus too going with him in his toga and hiding a short sword under his left arm, although he had sent a herald ahead in vain to call the slaves to freedom, seized the temple of Diana as a citadel. Against them D. Brutus, a man of consular rank, rushed in from the Publician slope with a great struggle. There Flaccus fought long and most stubbornly; Gracchus, after he had withdrawn into the temple of Minerva, wishing to fall on his sword, was held back by the intervention of Laetorius. And so, when the fight had long been carried on in doubt, at last archers sent by Opimius broke up the packed multitude. The two Flacci, father and son, when they had leapt down through the temple of Luna into a private house and barred the doors, were run through after the wattled wall was cut open. Gracchus, while his friends long fought for him and perished, barely reached the Sublician bridge, and there, so as not to be taken alive, offered his neck to his slave. Gracchus's head was cut off and brought to the consul; his body was carried to his mother Cornelia at the town of Misenum. This Cornelia, daughter of the elder Africanus, had withdrawn to Misenum, as I have said, at the death of her first son. Gracchus's goods were confiscated; the young Flaccus was put to death in the dungeon; of Gracchus's faction 250 are said to have been cut down on the Aventine. The consul Opimius, as he was brave in war, so he was cruel in the inquiry. For he put to death more than 3,000 men by punishments, of whom very many were innocent and killed without even pleading their case.

## 5.13

At the same time Metellus overran the Balearic islands in war and tamed them, and he put down, with very great slaughter of the inhabitants, the pirate raids that were then arising from them. Gnaeus Domitius too, as proconsul, defeated the Allobroges, a Gallic people, near the town of Vindalium in a very heavy war, above all because the enemy's horses and the enemy themselves fled in terror at the new shape of the elephants: 20,000 of the Allobroges are said to have been cut down there, and 3,000 were taken. At the same time Mount Etna blazed beyond its custom, and with fiery torrents poured over it and flowing far around it overwhelmed the city of Catina and its territory, so that the roofs of the houses, burned through and weighed down by hot ashes, fell in: to relieve this disaster the senate remitted the Catinians their taxes for ten years.

## 5.14

In the 628th year from the founding of the city the consul Fabius met with a small army Bituitus, king of the Arverni, a Gallic state, who was preparing war with the greatest equipment, so that Bituitus boasted that the small number of the Romans would hardly be enough as food for the dogs that he had in his column. When he saw that one bridge over the river Rhone was too little for taking his forces across, he built another of skiffs fastened together and joined with chains, with planks laid over and fixed on. When battle was joined and long hard fought, the Gauls were defeated and turned to flight; and while each feared for himself, their columns crowding together without thought, by their too hasty crossing of the bridge they broke its bonds and soon sank with the skiffs themselves. There are said to have been 180,000 armed men in the army of Bituitus, of whom 150,000 were either cut down or drowned. The consul Q. Marcius attacked in war a people of the Gauls set at the foot of the Alps. When they saw themselves hemmed in by the Roman forces and understood that they would be unequal to the war, they killed their wives and children and threw themselves into the flames. Those who, because the Romans got there first, had not then had the chance of carrying out their deaths and had been taken, made away with themselves, some by the sword, some by hanging, some by refusing food, and not one at all, not even a small child, was left alive to bear the condition of slavery for love of life.

## 5.15

In the 635th year from the founding of the city, in the consulship of P. Scipio Nasica and L. Calpurnius Bestia, the senate, with the consent of the Roman people, declared war on Jugurtha, king of the Numidians. But I shall touch briefly on Jugurtha only for the sake of the order and as a matter of record, since, about his varied and unbearable nature as about his deeds, done as craftily as they were vigorously, there is sufficient knowledge among all because of the rich brilliance of the writers. So Jugurtha, adopted by Micipsa, king of the Numidians, and made heir among his natural sons, first killed his co-heirs, that is Hiempsal, and drove Adherbal, defeated in war, out of Africa. Next he bribed with money the consul Calpurnius, sent against him, and brought him to most shameful terms of peace. Besides, when he had come to Rome himself, having either bribed or tried all with money, he stirred up seditions and quarrels; and when he left it he branded it with a notorious saying: "O city for sale, and soon to perish, if it finds a buyer!" In the following year he crushed in war A. Postumius, brother of the consul Postumius, whom the consul had put in command of an army of 40,000 armed men, as he gaped after the royal treasures stored at the city of Calama, and exacted a most ignominious treaty from him when he was defeated. He joined almost the whole of Africa, as it fell away from the Romans, to his kingdom. Afterwards, however, held in check by the integrity and discipline of the consul Metellus, and also defeated in two battles, he saw his Numidia laid waste in his own presence and unable to be defended: forced by him to surrender, he gave three hundred hostages, promised that he would pay corn and other supplies, and gave back more than three thousand deserters. Then, when, unsettled in peace, he did not hold back from wicked raids, he was broken by the cunning of the consul C. Marius, hardly less than that with which he himself was endowed, and by Roman strength, above all after Marius by a trick surrounded and took the city of Capsa, founded, as they say, by the Phoenician Hercules, and then most full of the royal treasures. Distrusting next his own affairs and strength, Jugurtha made an alliance with Bocchus, king of the Moors, and, enormously increased by his cavalry, wore out the army of Marius with very frequent raids. Last, at Cirta, an ancient city, the royal seat of Masinissa, he met the Romans as they were preparing to storm it, drawn up with 60,000 horsemen. Never was there a more tumultuous and terrible battle for the Roman soldier, so that the dust raised by the charging and the din of the horsemen riding round and attacking veiled the sky, took away the day and drew night over it, and so great a cloud of missiles fell on them that no part of the body was safe from a blow, since they lacked both sight to look ahead, because of the hindrance of the darkness, and freedom of movement to guard themselves, because of the pressure of the crowd. Nor did the Moorish and Numidian horseman take trouble to seek out an enemy well placed with a timely throw of his weapon, but rather they sent their javelins at random, sure that the wounds would not be at random. So the Roman foot soldiers, forced together, packed close. Night coming on gave an interval in so great a danger. On the next day the same face of war and of danger: the soldier, though his sword was drawn, could not break out against the enemy, for he was driven back from a distance by javelins; they could not flee, for the horsemen, swifter at pursuing, had shut them in on every side. Now the third day, and no help from anywhere, the dreadful face of death was set before them on every side: at last the consul Marius made a way for hope by a brave despair, burst out of the rampart with his whole column at once, and gave himself to the field and to battle together. And when the enemy, pouring round them again, were not only tearing the rear of the column but also cutting down its middle with missiles hurled from far off, and the heat of the sun, the unbearableness of thirst and death standing all round were besides wearing out the confused Romans to the last point of despair, suddenly that well-known help of storms and rains for the Romans against the Africans, sent from heaven, was an unhoped-for salvation. For a sudden rain gave refreshment and drink to the thirsty and burning Romans, and on the other hand made slippery, and so useless, the shafts of the Numidians' spears, which they are used to hurling by hand without throwing-thongs; their shields too, which they carried, handy and safe, of elephant hide stretched and hardened (whose nature is such that it drinks in rain it receives like a sponge, and so becomes unmanageable by its sudden weight), they could not defend themselves with, because they could not be swung about. So the Moors and Numidians, thrown into confusion and left helpless beyond expectation, Bocchus and Jugurtha fled. After this 90,000 armed men were thrown in by the same kings in a last battle; these too are said to have been cut down to extermination, the Romans winning. From then on Bocchus, casting off hope of war, sought peace, and as the price of peace he took Jugurtha by a trick and, loaded with chains, sent him through the legate Sulla to Marius. He was driven before the chariot in the triumph with his two sons, and soon strangled in prison. In the same days a foul and sad prodigy was seen. L. Helvius, a Roman knight, returning from Rome into Apulia with his wife and daughter, was caught by a storm; when he saw his daughter terrified, in order to reach the nearer roofs more quickly, they left the carriages and took to horses, and he took his maiden daughter, sitting on a horse, into the middle of the column. The girl was at once killed by a stroke of lightning, but all her clothes were taken off her without any tear, the bands of her breast and feet loosed, her necklaces and rings too knocked off, and her body itself unharmed, except that she lay naked in a shameful way with her tongue put out a little; the horse too that she was riding lay dead far off, its trappings, bridle and girths loosed and scattered here and there. A short interval of time after this L. Veturius, a Roman knight, defiled the Vestal virgin Aemilia in secret lust. Besides, the same Aemilia enticed two Vestal virgins to share in the unchastity, and exposed and handed them over to the companions of her seducer. When information was laid through a slave, punishment was taken on them all. In the same times of the Jugurthine war, besides, the consul L. Cassius pursued the Tigurini in Gaul as far as the Ocean, and in turn was surrounded by the same people in an ambush and killed; Lucius Piso too, a man of consular rank, legate of the consul Cassius, was killed. C. Publius, the other legate, so that the remaining part of the army, which had fled into the camp, should not be destroyed, gave hostages and half of everything to the Tigurini in a most shameful treaty: on returning to Rome, when a day for trial had been set by Caelius, tribune of the plebs, because he had given hostages to the Tigurini, he fled into exile. The proconsul Caepio, having captured a city of the Gauls whose name is Tolosa, took away from the temple of Apollo 100,000 pounds of gold and 110,000 of silver: when he had sent it with guards to Massilia, a city friendly to the Roman people, he is said to have had those to whom he had entrusted its guarding and carrying secretly killed, as some testify, and to have stolen it all by crime. From this too a great inquiry was afterwards held at Rome.

## 5.16

In the 642nd year from the founding of the city the consul C. Manlius and the proconsul Q. Caepio were sent against the Cimbri, Teutones, Tigurini and Ambrones, Gallic and German peoples, who had then conspired to wipe out the Roman Empire, and they divided their provinces between them with the river Rhone in the middle. There, while they quarrelled with each other in the heaviest jealousy and contention, they were defeated, with great disgrace and danger to the Roman name. For in that battle M. Aemilius, a man of consular rank, was taken and killed, and two sons of the consul were cut down; Antias writes that 80,000 Romans and allies were butchered at that time and 40,000 camp servants and sutlers killed. So of the whole army only ten men are said to have survived, to carry back the wretched news to increase the miseries. The enemy, taking both camps and enormous spoil, destroyed everything they had taken by a new and unusual kind of curse: clothing was torn up and thrown away, gold and silver thrown into the river, the men's breastplates hacked to pieces, the horses' trappings ruined, the horses themselves plunged into the whirlpools, and men were hanged from trees with nooses put round their necks, so that the victor recognised no spoil and the vanquished no mercy. At Rome then there was the greatest grief, and fear too, that the Cimbri would at once cross the Alps and destroy Italy. At the same time Q. Fabius Maximus killed his young son, whom he had banished to the country, with two slaves as agents of the murder, and at once freed those same slaves as the price of the crime. When a day of trial was set, with Cn. Pompeius prosecuting, he was condemned. So when Marius, consul for the fourth time, had pitched camp near the rivers Isère and Rhone, where they flow together, the Teutones, Cimbri, Tigurini and Ambrones fought around the Roman camp for three days on end, to see if they could somehow drive them out of their rampart and pour them out onto the plain, and then they resolved to make for Italy in three columns. Marius, after the enemy had left, moved camp and seized a hill that overlooked the plain and the river where the enemy had spread themselves out. And when his army lacked water to drink and he was reproached by the complaints of all, he answered that water was indeed in sight, but that it had to be claimed with the sword. So, as the camp servants rushed first into the fight with a shout, the army followed, and soon the war was carried on in a regular contest with ordered ranks, and the Romans won. On the fourth day the lines were again led out on both sides onto the plain and fought until midday with almost equal fortune. Afterwards, when the sun grew hot and the soft bodies of the Gauls melted away like snow, slaughter rather than battle was dragged on until night. 200,000 armed men were killed in that war, 80,000 taken, and barely 3,000 are said to have fled; their leader Teutobodus too was killed. Their women, with a more steadfast spirit than if they had won, asked the consul that, if they might serve the holy virgins and the gods with their chastity unviolated, he would keep them alive. And when they did not obtain what they asked, they dashed their little children against the rocks and all made away with themselves by the sword and by hanging. These things were done concerning the Tigurini and the Ambrones. But the Teutones and Cimbri, with their forces whole, had crossed the snows of the Alps and spread over the plains of Italy; and there, while that hardy race was long softened by milder air, drink, food and baths, Marius, consul for the fifth time, and Catulus were sent against them; when a day and a field had been given for battle, they followed the craft of Hannibal: they drew up for battle in a mist and fought in the sun. For the first confusion of the Gauls was that they ran into the Roman line drawn up before they noticed it was there. And when their horsemen, wounded at once, were forced back on their own men and threw into confusion the whole multitude still coming up in disorder, and the sun had risen with the wind and flashed in their faces, dust filled their sight and the glare dulled it. So it came about that so great and so terrible a multitude was cut down with the smallest loss to the Romans and its own utter extermination. 140,000 of them are said to have been cut down in battle then, and 60,000 taken. The women stirred up an almost heavier fight: they built the wagons round like a camp and themselves fought from on top, and long drove back the Romans. But when they were terrified by a new kind of slaughter (for they were left disfigured by a shameful enough wound, the crowns of their heads being cut off with the hair), they turned the steel that they had taken up against the enemy on themselves and their own. For some had their throats cut by each other in a mutual rush, some were strangled seizing each other by the throat, some, after ropes had been tied round the horses' legs and the horses themselves at once goaded on, put their own necks into the same ropes with which they had bound the horses' legs, and were dragged along and killed; some hanged themselves with a noose from the raised poles of the wagons. One woman was even found who had tied her two sons to her feet with nooses passed round their necks, and when she let herself drop to die by hanging, dragged them with her to be killed. Among these many and pitiful kinds of death two chieftains too are said to have run at each other with swords drawn. The kings Lugius and Boiorix fell in the battle; Claodicus and Caesorix were taken. So in these two battles 340,000 Gauls were killed and 140,000 taken, apart from the countless multitude of women, who killed themselves and their little ones with a woman's frenzy but a man's strength. So an unbelievable crime, never before known to the Romans, suddenly committed at Rome, darkened such a triumph of Marius and the Roman victory, the whole city being turned to horror and grief. For Publicius Malleolus, with his slaves helping, killed his own mother; he was condemned for parricide, sewn into a sack and thrown into the sea; and the Romans fulfilled both the crime and its penalty, about which Solon of Athens too had not dared to make a law, since he did not believe it could happen; and the Romans, who knew that they were sprung from Romulus, understanding that even this could happen, laid down a singular punishment.

## 5.17

In the 645th year from the founding of the city, after the Cimbric and Teutonic war and the fifth consulship of Marius, by which the state of the Roman Empire is judged to have been rightly preserved, in the sixth consulship of the same C. Marius it was so shaken that it almost fell to the last point by an internal disaster. To unroll and run through the windings of the quarrels and the inextricable causes of the seditions seems to me both awkward and long; let it be enough to have touched on them briefly: that L. Apuleius Saturninus came forward as the first author of the uproar that was stirred up, the bitterest enemy of Q. Metellus Numidicus, a man truly of the first rank. When Metellus was made censor, Saturninus dragged him from his house, and when he fled to the Capitol, besieged him with an armed multitude, from which Saturninus was driven down by the indignation of the Roman knights, after very great slaughter had been done before the Capitol. Next Saturninus and Glaucia, by the treachery of the consul C. Marius, killed A. Nunnius, Saturninus's rival for office. In the following year Marius, consul for the sixth time, Glaucia the praetor and Saturninus the tribune of the plebs conspired to drive Metellus Numidicus into exile by whatever force. When a day for trial had been set, Metellus, innocent, was condemned by crime by judges of the same faction put in place, and went into exile to the grief of the whole city. The same Saturninus, fearing that Memmius, a keen and upright man, would be made consul, raised a sudden sedition and, as Memmius fled, had him killed by his henchman P. Mettius, beaten to pieces with a shapeless club. As the senate and the Roman people roared at such great evils of the commonwealth, the consul Marius, with a talent suited to the times, mixed himself into the agreement of good men and calmed the stirred-up plebs with a gentle speech. Saturninus, with infamous daring, held an assembly at his own house, and there he was called king by some and imperator by others. Marius, having enrolled the plebs by maniples, posted the other consul with guards on the hill and himself fortified the gates. Battle was joined in the forum; Saturninus, driven from the forum by the Marians, fled to the Capitol; Marius cut the pipes by which water was brought there. Then a horrid enough fight was carried on at the entrance of the Capitol, and many were cut down around Saufeius and Saturninus. Saturninus, crying out openly, declared that Marius was the author of all his schemes; but when Saturninus himself, Saufeius and Labienus, compelled by Marius, had fled into the senate house, they were killed by Roman knights, who broke down the doors. C. Glaucia was dragged from the house of Claudius and butchered. Furius, tribune of the plebs, decreed that the goods of them all be confiscated. Cn. Dolabella, Saturninus's brother, fleeing through the vegetable market, was killed with L. Giganius. So, the authors of so great a sedition having been killed, there was quiet for the people. Then Cato and Pompeius brought forward a bill for the return of Metellus Numidicus, to the joy of the whole city: it was vetoed by the factions of the consul Marius and the tribune Furius, so that it should not be carried. Rutilius too, a most upright man, showed such constancy of faith and innocence that, when a day of trial was set for him by his accusers, up to the hearing he neither let his hair or beard grow, nor won over supporters by mourning dress or humble bearing, nor soothed his enemies, nor softened the judges; and even the speech allowed him by the praetor he made no more submissive than his spirit. Although he was attacked by plain false accusation and in the opinion of all good men was thought rightly to be acquitted, he was condemned by the perjury of the judges. He moved to Smyrna, and grew old there, devoted to the study of letters.

## 5.18

In the 659th year from the founding of the city, in the consulship of Sex. Julius Caesar and L. Marcius Philippus, the whole of Italy stirred up the Social War from internal causes. For Livius Drusus, tribune of the plebs, having enticed all the Latins with the hope of liberty, when he could not fulfil what he had promised, roused them to arms. To this was added that dreadful prodigies terrified the sad city. For at sunrise a ball of fire flashed out from the region of the north with a very great crash of the sky. Among the Arretines, when loaves were broken at banquets, gore flowed from the middle of the loaves as if from wounds of bodies. Besides, for seven days on end a hail of stones, with fragments of potsherds mixed in, beat the earth over a very wide area. Among the Samnites flame burst out of a vast gaping of the earth and was seen to stretch right up to the sky. Besides, many Romans on a journey saw a ball of a golden colour roll down from the sky to the earth, and, grown larger, be carried again from the earth on high towards the rising sun, and cover the sun itself with its size. Drusus, anxious at such great evils, was killed in his own house, by an unknown hand. So the Picentes, Vestini, Marsi, Paeligni, Marrucini, Samnites and Lucani, while they were still planning a secret revolt, killed at Asculum the praetor C. Servius, sent to them as envoy, and at once shut the city and cut the throats of all the Roman citizens, slaughter being proclaimed. At once most notorious prodigies went before the most atrocious ruin. For animals of all kinds, which were used to bearing the hands of men gently and living among men, left their stalls and pastures and fled to the woods and mountains with pitiful bleating, neighing and lowing. Dogs too, whose nature it is not to be able to live apart from men, wandered about like wolves with tearful howling. So the praetor Cn. Pompeius waged war with the Picentes by order of the senate and was defeated, after the Samnites had set Papius Mutilus over them as commander and the Marsi had chosen the arch-pirate Agamemnon. Julius Caesar, defeated in battle by the Samnites, fled with his army cut down. The consul Rutilius chose his kinsman Marius as his legate: when Marius constantly advised that delay would be useful in the war and that the raw soldiers ought to be exercised in camp for a little, he thought Marius did it with guile, scorned him, and threw himself, off his guard, with the whole column of his army into an ambush of the Marsi, where the consul himself was killed, many nobles were slain, and 8,000 Roman soldiers were cut down. The river Tolenus carried the arms and bodies of the slain within sight of Marius the legate and bore them out as evidence of the disaster. Marius, at once gathering his forces, crushed the victors unexpectedly and killed 8,000 of the Marsi himself. But Caepio, led into an ambush by the Vestini and Marsi, was butchered with his army. L. Julius Caesar, after he had fled when defeated at Aesernia, gathered forces from everywhere, fought against the Samnites and Lucani, and killed many thousands of the enemy. And when he had been hailed imperator by the army and had sent messengers to Rome about the victory, the senate, at this smiling hope, laid aside the military cloaks, that is the dress of mourning, which it had put on when the Social War broke out, and recovered the ancient dignity of the toga. Marius then cut down 6,000 of the Marsi and stripped 7,000 of their arms. Sulla, sent with twenty-four cohorts to Aesernia, where Roman citizens and soldiers were pressed by a very close siege, saved the city and the allies with a very great battle and very great slaughter of the enemy. Cn. Pompeius routed the Picentes in a heavy battle; by this victory the senate took back the broad stripe and the other insignia of rank, having put on only the togas when it first breathed again at Caesar's victory. The praetor Porcius Cato defeated the Etruscans and the legate Plotius the Umbrians, with very much blood spent and with the hardest labour. In the consulship of Cn. Pompeius and L. Porcius Cato, Pompeius long besieged the city of Asculum; and yet he would not have stormed it had he not defeated the people, as they burst out onto the plain, with the heaviest crushing. 18,000 of the Marsi were cut down in that battle with their commander Fraucus, and 3,000 taken. But 4,000 Italian men, fleeing from that slaughter in a column forced together, happened to climb the ridge of a mountain, where, overwhelmed and killed by the snows, they stiffened in a pitiful death. For just as they had stood, stunned by fear of the enemy, some leaning against tree stumps or rocks, some resting on their arms, all were seen with their eyes open and their teeth bared like living men; nor was there any sign of death to those looking from far off except their long stillness, which the vigour of human life can in no way endure for long. On the same day the Picentes joined battle and were defeated; their leader Vidacilius called together his chief men, and after a magnificent banquet and generous drinking, challenging them all to follow his example, drank poison and died, all praising his deed but none following it. In the 661st year from the founding of the city, when a Roman army had gone to besiege Pompeii, Postumius Albinus, a man of consular rank and then legate of L. Sulla, stirred up the hatred of all the soldiers against himself by his unbearable arrogance and was killed with stones. The consul Sulla declared that citizen blood could be expiated only by the blood of the enemy: moved by its awareness of this, the army attacked the battle in such a way that each man saw he must perish unless he won. 18,000 Samnites were cut down in that battle; he also pursued and killed Juventius, an Italian general, and a great body of his people. The consul Porcius Cato, who had the Marian forces, after he had done some things vigorously, boasted that C. Marius had done no greater things, and for this, when he was waging war against the Marsi at the Fucine lake, he was struck down by the son of C. Marius in the confusion of battle, as if by an unknown hand. The legate C. Gabinius was killed in the storming of an enemy camp. The Marrucini and Vestini were laid waste, Sulpicius, Pompeius's legate, pursuing them. Popaedius and Obsidius, Italian commanders, were crushed and killed by the same Sulpicius at the river Teanus in a horrible battle. Pompeius, entering Asculum, had the prefects, centurions and all their chief men beaten with rods and struck with the axe, sold the slaves and all the spoil at auction, and ordered the rest to go away free indeed, but naked and in want; and though the senate hoped that there would be some help from this spoil for the use of public pay, Pompeius gave nothing from it to the needy treasury. For at the same time, when the treasury had been utterly drained and money was lacking for the pay in corn, the public places around the Capitol that had been given into the possession of the pontiffs, augurs, decemvirs and flamens were sold under the pressure of want, and a sufficient sum of money was received to help for the time of want. Indeed at that time the wealth of all the overthrown cities and stripped lands, scraped off from every side, was being heaped into the lap of that city itself, while Rome herself, driven by shameful want, was auctioning the chief parts of herself. So let her consider her times then, when, like an insatiable belly consuming everything and always hungry, more wretched herself than all the cities she made wretched, leaving nothing, she had nothing, and was thrust by the goad of hunger at home into continuing the restlessness of war. At the same time King Sothimus, entering Greece with great Thracian auxiliaries, laid waste all the territory of Macedonia, and at last, overcome by the praetor C. Sentius, was forced to return to his kingdom.

## 5.19

In the 662nd year from the founding of the city, with the Social War not yet finished, the first civil war was stirred up at Rome, and in the same year the Mithridatic war, though less infamous, yet no less heavy, was begun. Indeed it has been handed down variously about the length of the Mithridatic war, whether it first began from this point or then chiefly blazed up, above all since some say it was waged for thirty years and others for forty. But although things done at the same times blazed up heaped together with tangled evils, yet I shall set them out one by one, even if briefly. Marius, when Sulla was consul and about to set out with an army into Asia against Mithridates, but was halting in Campania because of the remains of the Social War, aimed at a seventh consulship and at taking on the Mithridatic war. When Sulla learned of this, impatient, a young man in truth, and stirred by unrestrained anger, he first encamped with four legions before the city, where he killed Gratidius, Marius's legate, as if the first victim of the civil war; soon he burst into the city with the army and called for torches to set the city on fire. While all hid in fear, he came into the forum with a swift column by the Sacred Way. Marius, having tried in vain to stir the nobility, inflame the plebs, and last to arm the equestrian order against Sulla, at last called the slaves to arms with the hope of liberty and plunder, dared to resist to no purpose, and finally withdrew to the Capitol. But when Sulla's cohorts burst in there, he fled with great slaughter of his men. Then Sulpicius, Marius's colleague, was struck down there, his own slave betraying him; the consuls decreed that the slave himself should be freed because he had informed on an enemy, but thrown from the Tarpeian rock because he had betrayed his master. Marius, fleeing, when he had been hemmed in by the pressure of his pursuers, hid himself in the marshes of Minturnae; from which he was unhappily dragged out, smeared with mud, and ignominiously led to Minturnae in a shameful sight and thrust into prison, where he terrified by his look alone the assassin sent to him. Then, slipping out of his chains, he fled over to Africa, and, having called his son from Utica, where he was being watched in custody, at once returned to Rome and joined the consul Cinna in a partnership of crimes. So, to bring down the whole commonwealth, they divided their army into four parts. For three legions were given to Marius, Cn. Carbo was put over part of the forces, Sertorius received a part, that same Sertorius, already here an inciter and partner of civil war, who even when this was finished afterwards stirred up another war in Spain, which he dragged out for many years with the greatest disasters for the Romans. The rest of the army followed Cinna. Then Cn. Pompeius, who had been summoned with his army by the senate to help the commonwealth, and had long held back, watching for revolution, scorned by Marius and Cinna, went over to Octavius, the other consul, and soon clashed with Sertorius. The coming of night broke off the unhappy battle; six hundred soldiers were butchered on each side. On the next day, as the bodies, mixed together, were being sorted for burial, a soldier of Pompeius recognised the body of his brother, whom he himself had killed: for in the clash the helmet had taken away from both the recognition of the face, and fury the looking; although there is little guilt in ignorance, so that he may seem not to have known about his brother, who without doubt knew about the citizen. And so the victor, more unhappy than the vanquished, when he recognised both his brother's body and his own murder of kin, cursed civil wars, at once drove his sword through his breast, and, pouring out tears and blood together, threw himself on his brother's corpse. And what good did it do to shame that cruel undertaking, that at the very first beginning of the civil wars the infamous report spread that brothers had clashed, unknowing indeed, but knowing that they were citizens; that the brother, victor by a crime, had sought the spoils of the brother he had killed, and soon, guilty of so great a monstrousness, by the same sword and the same hand had avenged by his own death the murder of kin he had committed? Did so sad an example move the passions of the parties set on war? Did the terror of an error drive off the danger of a crime from anyone? Did this piety and reverence for nature, which is shared even with the beasts, [...] what one man committed by killing and dying, because it could be done to himself, tremble, and, overcome by conscience, withdraw from such an undertaking? Rather, for almost forty years following, civil wars were continued to such a degree that the greatness of praise was thought to be weighed by the greatness of the crime. For after such a lesson all would have shunned the dangers of murdering kin in such a warfare, had they not wanted the murders of kin themselves. So Marius, entering the colony of Ostia by force, practised there every kind of lust, greed and cruelty. Pompeius was struck by lightning and died; and his army, seized by a pestilence, was almost wholly consumed. For 11,000 men died from Pompeius's camp, and 6,000 were struck down from the side of the consul Octavius. Marius burst into the cities of Antium and Aricia as an enemy, killed everyone in them except the traitors, and let his men plunder their goods. Afterwards the consul Cinna with the legions and Marius with the runaways entered the city and killed all the noblest men of the senate and very many men of consular rank. But how small a part is this of the misery displayed? [...] to have defined in one word the slaughter of good men, of which there was such great number, such length, such cruelty and such variety? Yet it is fairer that I should have withdrawn something of usefulness from the case than to have heaped so much horror on its telling, whether these things are set before the skilled or the unskilled. For we are speaking of our country, of our citizens and of our forefathers, who, driven by these evils, did such abominable things that even their descendants shudder at hearing them, who certainly do not want them too much exaggerated, either for the restraint of sufficient knowledge, if they know them, or from regard for merciful reverence, if they do not. So Marius, while he heaped up the heads of slain citizens, brought to banquets, offered to Capitols, gathered on the Rostra for show and ornament, and had seized a seventh consulship with Cinna consul for the third time, was at last carried off, at the beginning of his consular power, by a late death. Cinna made good the killings of good men by slaughter of bad. For since the band of runaways brought in by Marius was insatiable for plunder and gave no share to the consuls, the authors of the plunder, it was enticed into the forum as if for pay, surrounded by soldiers, and destroyed unarmed. 8,000 runaways were cut down that day in the forum of the city; and the same Cinna, consul for the fourth time, was killed by his own army.

## 5.20

Meanwhile the remnant of the senators, who had escaped by flight the power of Cinna, the cruelty of Marius, the madness of Fimbria and the boldness of Sertorius, crossed over into Greece and compelled Sulla with prayers to bring help to their country in danger, indeed already almost lost. So Sulla, as soon as he touched the shore of Campania, crushed the consul Norbanus in battle: 7,000 Romans were then killed by Romans, 6,000 of them were taken by the same, and 124 fell on Sulla's side. Fabius Hadrianus, who held command as propraetor, aiming at the kingship of Africa with a band of slaves, was burned alive with his whole household at Utica by their masters, on a heap of brushwood piled up. The praetor Damasippus, at the instigation of the consul Marius, most cruelly killed Q. Scaevola, C. Carbo, L. Domitius and P. Antistius, called into the senate house as if for consultation. The bodies of the slain were dragged with a hook by executioners and thrown into the Tiber. At the same time Sulla's generals fought very many battles against the Marian party with a most unhappy happiness. For Q. Metellus cut down the forces of Carrinas and seized his camp, and Cn. Pompeius heavily butchered Carbo's cavalry. There was also then a very great battle of Sulla and the young Marius at Sacriportus, in which 25,000 of Marius's army were cut down, as Claudius writes. Pompeius also stripped Carbo of his camp and, pursuing him as he fled, now by cutting down and now by forcing to surrender, deprived him of the greatest part of his army. Metellus crushed the column of Norbanus: there 9,000 of the Marian party were killed. Lucullus, when he was besieged by Quintius, broke out and destroyed the besieging army in a sudden fight. For more than 10,000 are said to have been cut down there then. Then Sulla joined battle with Camponius, the leader of the Samnites, and the remaining forces of Carrinas before the city itself and the Colline gate, up to the ninth hour of the day, and at last won in a very heavy battle. 80,000 men are said to have been routed there: 12,000 surrendered; the rest of the multitude, turned to flight, the insatiable anger of the victorious citizens consumed.

## 5.21

As soon as Sulla entered the city as victor, he killed 3,000 men who had surrendered through envoys, unarmed and unsuspecting, against right and against the faith given. Very many were also killed then, not to say innocent, but even of Sulla's own party, who they say were more than 9,000. So slaughter went on freely through the city, the assassins wandering everywhere as anger or plunder enticed each. So when all were now openly murmuring at what each feared, Q. Catulus said openly to Sulla: "With whom, then, are we to live, if in war we kill the armed and in peace the unarmed?" Then Sulla, at the suggestion of L. Fursidius, a chief centurion, first brought in that infamous list of proscription. The first proscription was of eighty men, among whom were four of consular rank, Carbo, Marius, Norbanus and Scipio, and among them Sertorius, then most to be feared. Another was likewise put up with five hundred names; and when Lollius, carefree and conscious of nothing against himself, was reading it, and suddenly came on his own name, while he slipped away from the forum in fear with his head covered, he was killed. But not even in the lists themselves did there seem to be any trust or end of the evils. For some whom they had proscribed they cut down, and others, after they had cut them down, they proscribed. Nor was there a single road to death itself or one condition, so that in the killing of citizens at least the law of enemies might be kept, who demand nothing of the conquered but their life. For Sulla ordered M. Marius, dragged out of a goat shed, to be bound, led across the Tiber to the tomb of the Lutatii, and there butchered, his eyes gouged out and his limbs cut off piece by piece or even broken. After him P. Laetorius, a senator, and Venuleius, a triumvir, were killed. M. Marius's head was sent to Praeneste: on seeing it C. Marius, seized by final despair, where he was being besieged by Lucretius, so as not to fall into the hands of his enemies, ran at Telesinus for a death at each other's hands. And while he himself drove his hands more violently at the man running at him, he dulled the hand of the one striking him around his own wound. So, when Telesinus was killed, he himself, lightly wounded, offered his neck to his slave. Sulla cut the throat of the praetor Carrinas. Setting out from there to Praeneste, he ordered all the chief men of the Marian army, that is the legates, quaestors, prefects and tribunes, to be killed. Pompeius killed Carbo, who was trying to flee from the island of Cossura to Egypt and was dragged back to him in Sicily, and many of his companions with him. Sulla was made dictator, so that his lust for domination and cruelty might be both armed and veiled by reverence for an honourable and eminent name. Pompeius crossed into Africa and, when a sally had been made near Utica, killed 18,000 men. In this war Domitius, the Marian general, was killed while fighting among the foremost. The same Pompeius pursued Hiarbas, king of Numidia, and as he fled had him stripped of all his forces by Bogud, son of Bocchus king of the Moors: and when Hiarbas at once returned to Bulla, the town was handed over to Pompeius and he killed him.

## 5.22

So when P. Servilius and Appius Claudius had been made consuls, Sulla was at last seen as a private citizen. With this end two most deadly wars were closed, the Italian Social War and Sulla's civil war. These, dragged out over ten years, consumed more than 150,000 Romans; and Rome lost in this civil war as many of her choicest men and home-born soldiers as the census found in her, in its several age groups, at an earlier time, when she was already looking about her against Alexander the Great; besides, twenty-four men of consular rank, six of praetorian, sixty of aedilician and almost two hundred senators, apart from the countless peoples of all Italy, who were wiped out everywhere without consideration; and let anyone deny, if he can, that Rome conquered with the same loss to herself as Italy lost. For shame! Do the times here too need a doubtful comparison? Yes indeed, most of all, they say: for what is so fitly compared as civil wars with civil wars? Or will it perhaps be said that there were no civil wars in these times too? To them we shall answer that these should more justly be called social wars, but that it profits us if they are called civil. For when all things are shown to be alike in causes, names and aims, then the reverence of the Christian religion claims so much the more for itself in them as the angry power of the victor has presumed the less. For when often wicked tyrants, rashly invading the commonwealth and usurping the royal state, have torn off a limb of the Roman Empire, and from it have either brought in wars unjust in themselves or stirred up wars just against themselves, made and equipped by the peoples of the Britons and Gauls: these wars, as near as they are to foreign wars, so far from civil ones, what should they rightly be called but social, since the Romans themselves never anywhere named even the wars of Sertorius or Perperna or Crixus or Spartacus civil? In such a revolt or treason of allies, then, there would surely now be less ill will, if perhaps a heavy battle or a bloody victory should arise. Yet since in these times all things bring more necessity and less shame, that is cause, battle and victory, whether to put down the insolence of tyrants, to hold back the revolt of allies, or to brand an example of vengeance: to whom, at last, is it doubtful how much more mildly and how much more mercifully civil wars, as they call them, once stirred up, are now carried on, or rather put down rather than carried on? For who will have heard of one civil war being carried on for ten years in these times? Who will remember 150,000 men cut down in one war, even of enemies by enemies, not to say of citizens by citizens? Who will have known that multitude, which it would be long to set out, of the best and most distinguished men butchered in peace? Last, who will have feared, read, felt those infamous lists of those to be killed? And is it not rather known to all that all, settled in one peace and secure in the same safety, the vanquished and victors alike, rejoiced in a common joy, and that in so many provinces, cities and peoples of the whole Roman Empire there have hardly ever been a few whom a just vengeance condemned, even against the victor's will? And, not to load words on words, I would not say without reason that as great a band of common soldiers was lately wiped out, at least in war, as was then cut down of nobles in peace. So when Sulla was dead, Lepidus, champion of the Marian party, rising against Catulus, Sulla's general, stirred up again the reviving ashes of the civil wars. They fought twice then in battle; very many Romans were cut down, wretched now in their very fewness and still raving with that madness. The city of the Albans, attacked by siege and tormented by the last famine, was saved by the surrender of its pitiful remnants; there Scipio, son of Lepidus, was then taken and killed. Brutus, fleeing into Cisalpine Gaul with Pompeius pursuing, was killed at Regium. So this civil war, not so much by the mercy of Catulus as by weariness of Sulla's cruelty, like fire in stubble, vanished as swiftly as it had blazed up.

## 5.23

In the 673rd year from the founding of the city, with the crash of wars sounding on every side, one of which was in Spain, another in Pamphylia, a third in Macedonia, a fourth in Dalmatia, the Roman commonwealth, still bloodless and exhausted by internal ruin as if by fevers, was forced to drive off with arms the bravest peoples of the West and North. For Sertorius, a man strong in guile and boldness, who had been of the Marian party, fleeing Sulla, slipped away from Africa into the Spains and roused the most warlike peoples to arms. Against him, to put it briefly, two generals were sent, Metellus and Domitius: of these Domitius was crushed with his army by Hirtuleius, Sertorius's general. Manlius, proconsul of Gaul, crossed into Spain with three legions and 1,500 horsemen and joined an unequal battle with Hirtuleius: stripped by him of camp and forces, he fled back almost alone to the town of Ilerda. Metellus, worn out by many battles, wandering through out-of-the-way places, wore out the enemy by delay, until he could join Pompeius's camp. Pompeius, having gathered an army at Palantia, tried in vain to defend the city of Lauro, which Sertorius was then attacking, and fled defeated. Sertorius, having overcome and routed Pompeius, took Lauro and laid it waste most bloodily; the rest of the people of Lauro, who had survived the slaughter, he took off into Lusitania in pitiful captivity. He boasted that he had defeated Pompeius, that is, that famous general of the Romans, whom Rome had sent to this war, endowed with great confidence, not as proconsul but in place of the consuls. Galba writes that Pompeius then had 30,000 foot and a thousand horse, and records that Sertorius had 60,000 foot and 8,000 horse. But afterwards Hirtuleius, joining battle with Metellus at Italica, a city of Baetica, lost 20,000 soldiers and fled back defeated with a few into Lusitania. Pompeius took Belgida, a famous city of Celtiberia. Then Sertorius, joining battle with Pompeius, killed 10,000 of his soldiers; Pompeius winning on the other wing, he himself lost almost as many. Many battles besides were fought between them. Memmius, Pompeius's quaestor and also his sister's husband, was killed. The brothers Hirtuleius were slain. Perperna, who had joined Sertorius, was crushed. Last, Sertorius himself, at length in the tenth year after the war began, killed by the treachery of his own men, like Viriathus, ended the war and gave the Romans a victory without glory; although part of his army afterwards followed Perperna: he was defeated by Pompeius and killed with his whole army. All the cities were received in surrender of their own accord and without delay; only two resisted, that is Uxama and Calagurris: of these Pompeius overthrew Uxama, and Afranius, having worn out Calagurris by a continuous siege and forced it by pitiful want to infamous food, destroyed it with final slaughter and fire. The assassins of Sertorius thought that a reward was not even to be asked of the Romans, since they remembered that it had been refused before to the assassins of Viriathus. And although they then achieved Roman security for no reward, yet Spain, always strong in faith and strength, while she has given the best and most unconquered kings to the commonwealth, from the beginning until this day has never sent out any tyrant sprung from herself, nor let any tyrant who came at her from outside go away alive or in power. Meanwhile Claudius, who had drawn the Macedonian war by lot, tried to drive out of the territory of Macedonia by war various peoples who are spread around the Rhodope mountains and were then most cruelly plundering Macedonia (for among other things horrible to say and hear that they did to captives, when they needed a cup, they would seize the bones of human heads, still bloody and hairy and smeared inside the hollows with brain badly dug out, and use them greedily and without horror as if they were real cups: of these peoples the most bloody and monstrous were the Scordisci); these, as I said, Claudius tried to drive out, and threw himself against great masses of evils: from which, sick in mind and hemmed in by cares, and besides seized by sickness, he died. His successor Scribonius, avoiding the strength of the peoples attacked in the earlier war, turned his arms on Dardania and overcame it. Publius Servilius, former consul, attacked Cilicia and Pamphylia most cruelly, and while he was eager to subdue them, almost destroyed them. He besieged, crushed and took Lycia and its cities. Besides, he roamed over Mount Olympus and overthrew Phaselis, destroyed Corycus, and searching out the sides of Mount Taurus too that slope towards Cilicia, he brought the Isaurians, broken in war, under his rule; he was the first of the Romans to lead an army through the Taurus, and made a road of the march. When the three years in which the war was waged were over, he took the name Isauricus. The proconsul Cosconius, who had drawn Illyricum by lot, having trampled down and subdued Dalmatia, at last stormed and took Salonae, a most flourishing city, after two years.

## 5.24

In the 679th year from the founding of the city, in the consulship of Lucullus and Cassius, seventy-four gladiators fled from the school of Cn. Lentulus at Capua: at once, under the leaders Crixus and Oenomaus, Gauls, and Spartacus, a Thracian, they seized Mount Vesuvius; bursting out from there, they stormed the camp of the praetor Clodius, who had surrounded them with a siege, and, having put him to flight, turned everything into plunder. Then, going round by Consentia and Metapontum, they soon collected enormous columns. For Crixus is said to have had a multitude of 10,000 then, and Spartacus three times the number. For Oenomaus had already been killed in an earlier battle. So, while they confounded everything with slaughter, burning, plunder and rape, at the funeral of a captive matron, who had killed herself in grief at her violated honour, they gave a gladiatorial show of four hundred captives, those namely who had been bound to watch now being watched, as trainers of gladiators rather than commanders of soldiers. Then the consuls Gellius and Lentulus were sent against them with an army; of them Gellius crushed Crixus in battle, though he fought most fiercely, and Lentulus, overcome by Spartacus, fled. Afterwards, both consuls having joined their forces in vain, they suffered a heavy disaster and fled. Next the same Spartacus crushed the proconsul C. Cassius in war and killed him. So, the city being terrified with almost no less fear than it had trembled with under Hannibal roaring around the gates, the senate sent Crassus with the consuls' legions and a new levy of soldiers. As soon as he entered battle with the runaways, he killed 6,000 of them and took nine hundred. Then, before attacking in war Spartacus himself, who was pitching camp at the source of the river Silarus, he overcame his Gallic and German auxiliaries, of whom he killed 30,000 men together with their leaders. At last he met Spartacus himself in drawn-up battle and struck down him and very great forces of runaways with him. For 60,000 of them are said to have been cut down and 6,000 taken, and 3,000 Roman citizens were recovered. The rest, who had slipped away from this war and were wandering about, were worn down by many generals with frequent hunts. But I repeat again and again: do the times here too need any comparison? Who, I ask, would not shudder to hear, I will not say of such wars, but even such names of wars: foreign, servile, social, civil, against runaways? Which do not even follow one another as the waves of a stirred-up sea follow, however great their masses, but rush together from every side, stirred up and heaped together with different causes, names, forms and evils. To go back to the nearest and pass over that infamous servile war: the Jugurthine war had not yet finished thundering from the south-west, when the Cimbric was already flashing lightning from the north-west. From those Cimbric clouds foul and vast torrents of shed blood were still being driven along, when wretched Italy was already breathing out the mists of the Social War, which would at once gather into great storm clouds of evils. Still, after the endless and frequent storm of the Italian war, it was by no means possible to go about Italy safely, so much were all things tottering, apart from those most dangerous whirlpools of hostile cities, in a loose and slippery peace; already Rome was in labour with the ruin of Marius and Cinna, and from the other side the East and the North threatened another, that is the Mithridatic, which, begun earlier, stretches on further. From the torch of Marius the pyre of Sulla's disaster was lit; from that most deadly pyre of Sulla and the civil war burning stakes were scattered through very many parts of the earth and spread many fires from one kindling. For Lepidus and Scipio in Italy, Brutus in Gaul, Domitius, Cinna's son-in-law, in Africa, Carbo in Cossura and Sicily, Perperna in Liguria and afterwards with Sertorius in Spain, and the most atrocious of all, Sertorius, in that same Spain, stirring up these civil wars, or whatever other name they are to be called by, made many out of one and great ones out of a great one; apart from those three vast wars that were then called foreign, that is the Pamphylian, Macedonian and Dalmatian, and leaving aside that great Mithridatic war, far the most dangerous and most fearful of all; then, while the Spanish war of Sertorius was not yet finished, indeed while Sertorius himself was still alive, this war of runaways, or, to speak more truly, of gladiators, bristled up, no longer to be watched by a few but to be feared everywhere. Since it is called the war of runaways, let no one think it cheap from its name: often in it the consuls were defeated singly, and sometimes both together with their columns joined in vain, and very many nobles were butchered; and the runaways themselves who were cut down were more than a hundred thousand. From this we advise that Italy console herself for the harassment of the present foreigners by remembering the past, when her own people, sprung from her and at home in her, tore her apart incomparably more cruelly. So I will now make an end of this fifth volume, so that the civil wars mixed everywhere with foreign ones, both those that have been told and those that follow, since they have so clung together in the series of the times and in evils that followed one another, may at least be separated by the end of a book.

## 6.1

All men, of whatever sect or life or country, are always so raised by a natural good to the outlook of wisdom that, even if not in action, yet in judgement they know that the reason of the mind is to be preferred to the delight of the body. This mind, lit up with reason as its guide, rising among the virtues, to which it rises with an inborn favour, although it is inclined towards vices, looks out towards the knowledge of God as towards a citadel. For any man can despise God for a time; he cannot wholly fail to know Him. So some, while they believe God to be in many things, have made up many gods out of an undiscerning fear. But from this there has now been a departure, above all as the authority of the truth works, and as reason itself too sifts the matter. For even their philosophers, to say nothing of our saints, while they seek and search out all things with the intent study of the mind, have found one God, author of all things, to whom alone all things should be referred; so that even now the pagans, whom the truth, now declared, convicts of stubbornness rather than ignorance, when they are questioned by us, confess that they do not follow many gods but venerate many ministers under one great god. There remains, then, a confused disagreement about the understanding of the true God, through many guesses at understanding, because about one god almost all have one opinion. Human searching could reach this far, even if with labour. But where reasoning fails, faith comes to help. For unless we believe, we shall not understand: what you would know truly about God, you should hear from Him and believe God Himself.
