So that same one and true God, in whom every sect, as we have said, even if from different opinions, meets, changing kingdoms and ordering times, punishing sins too, chose the weak things of the world to confound the strong, and founded the Roman Empire, taking up a shepherd of the poorest condition. After it had long been advanced through kings and consuls and had taken possession of Asia, Africa and Europe, by His ordering He gathered all things together under one emperor, and he the bravest and most merciful. Under this emperor, whom almost all peoples justly honoured with mingled love and fear, the true God, who was worshipped by the ignorant with anxious superstition, opened that great spring of the understanding of Himself, and, so as to teach men more readily through a man, sent His Son, working miracles that surpassed man, and refuting the demons whom some had thought gods, so that those who had not believed Him as a man should believe His works as God's; then so that, in a great silence and the widest peace, the glory of the new name and the swift report of the salvation announced might run through without hindrance and quickly, and also so that His disciples, going through the various peoples and offering freely to all the gifts of salvation, might have a safe freedom to go about and to speak, since they were Roman citizens among Roman citizens. I have thought this should be mentioned because this sixth little book extends as far as Caesar Augustus, of whom these things are said. But if some think this most clear reasoning void, and assign it rather to their own gods, whom they say they first chose with wisdom and then invited with special worship, so that through them this most ample and most beautiful empire might be founded for them (for so they boast, that by the best kind of rites they earned the special favour of the gods, and that when these were taken away or neglected, "all the gods by whom this empire had stood departed, leaving their shrines and altars"), then, although the reverence of your holiness has discussed many things most strongly and most truly, yet the place demands that I too add a few. If the Romans earned the favour of the gods by worshipping them and lost it by not worshipping: that Romulus himself, the parent of Rome, should be safe amid so many evils pressing on him from his very birth, who earned this by worship? His grandfather Amulius, who exposed him to death? His father, who was uncertain? His mother Rhea, guilty of unchastity? The Albans, his kin, who also persecuted the very seeds of the Roman name from the beginning? All Italy, which for four hundred years, while it could dare, gaped after its ruin? No, they say, but the gods themselves, since they knew that they were to be worshipped, guarded their future worshippers. Then they foreknew. If they foreknew, why, among the ages of so many years, did they bring this empire to the highest citadel of power at that time above all in which He willed to be born among men and to be known as a man, after whose name they themselves were despised as nothing, and even those whom they had advanced ran with the whole world to Him? But He crept in humbly, they say, and entered secretly. If He was secret and humble, whence so famous a report, so undoubted a faith, so manifest a power? By certain signs and miracles, they say, He seized and held the minds of men, anxious with superstition. But if a man could do these things, the gods ought to have been able to do more. Or, because He preached that this power had been handed to Him by the Father, did men at some time come to the understanding of that known and unknown God, which, as I said, no one can grasp except through Him? Nor can anyone, except one who, having looked through and looked down on his whole self, has turned to the wisdom of God and carried over all the reasoning of seeking into the faith of believing. Yet I discuss it briefly: those gods, whom they call so great that they seem both to have advanced the Roman commonwealth when favourable and to have afflicted it when turned away, were certainly, as is openly agreed, worshipped most devoutly and most intently at the time when Christ willed to be born and began to be announced to the peoples. So were they, taking thought for themselves and their worshippers, unable either to check or to drive back His religion, on account of which they saw that they would be spurned and their worshippers forsaken? And yet, if they were forsaken by the unwilling, pardon should be given and they should not have been deserted; if by the willing, their foreknowledge should have been kept and they should not have been helped before. This was done, they say; for we stirred up peoples, inflamed kings, set up laws, appointed judges, prepared penalties of tortures and crosses, searched the whole world, to see whether by any means the Christian name and worship could be scraped off the whole world. This was done until a fruitful cruelty advanced so far amid torments and through torments that it took possession of the royal summit itself, through which alone it could have been prevented. And what followed afterwards? The Christian emperors, they say, ordered the rites to cease and the temples to be shut, and so "all the gods by whom this empire had stood departed, leaving their shrines and altars." O how great and how unobstructed is the light of truth, if weak eyes did not unhappily close against it as it offers itself freely! If the Christian faith could in no way be held down through many past times, while peoples, kings, laws, slaughters, crosses and deaths raged against it on every side, but rather, as I said, grew amid these and through these, while the worship of idols, already in a way failing of itself and blushing at itself, ceased at a single most merciful command without any terror of punishment: to whom is it doubtful that by this demonstration of that understanding the creature at last came to know about its creator what it had until then sought through the various reasonings of the mind, however intent, darkened by other things, and so at once clung to the love of Him whom it had loved even without knowing? Nor is it to be wondered at, then, if in a great household some slaves are found who, used to the habit and wantonness of those who lead them astray, abuse their master's patience to the point of despising him: so God rightly rebukes the ungrateful or the unbelieving or even the stubborn with various chastisements; which must indeed always be confessed, but above all for that time when there was as yet no Church throughout the whole world to soften, by the intervention of faithful prayers, the deserved punishments of the world and the just judgement of God, His mercy being entreated: so these things too, which seem evils to men, whatever they are, were all without doubt heavier then, as will be proved in the very order in which they began. The Mithridatic war, or, to speak more truly, the disasters of the Mithridatic war, involving many provinces at once, was dragged out and stretched over forty years. For it blazed up, as I said, in the 662nd year from the founding of the city, in which the first civil war too had begun, and in the consulship of Cicero and Antonius, to speak in the words of the best of poets, it was barely finished "by barbarous poison". But in these times thirty years of waging war are found. How it has been called forty by most is not easily made out.

## 6.2

So Mithridates, king of Pontus and Armenia, after he had tried to strip Nicomedes, king of Bithynia, a friend of the Roman people, of his kingdom, and had been warned by the senate that, if he attempted it, war would be brought against him by the Roman people, in anger at once invaded Cappadocia, drove out its king Ariobarzanes, and laid waste the whole province with fire and sword. Then he seized Bithynia with like disaster. He afflicted Paphlagonia with a similar fate, driving out its kings Pylaemenes and Nicomedes. Afterwards, when he had come to Ephesus, he ordered by a cruel edict that whatever Roman citizens were found throughout all Asia should all be killed on a single day. And it was done; and in no way can it be set out or grasped in words what a multitude of Roman citizens was then cut down, what grief there was in very many provinces, what groaning of those to be killed and of the killers alike, when every single person was forced either to betray innocent guests and friends or himself to risk the punishment meant for his guests. Archelaus too, Mithridates's general, sent ahead into Achaea with 120,000 foot and horse, took Athens and all Greece, partly by force and partly by surrender. Sulla, to whom the Mithridatic war had fallen after his consulship, long besieged Archelaus at the Piraeus, the port of the Athenians, fortified with a sevenfold wall, and took the city of the Athenians itself by force. Afterwards he clashed with Archelaus in a regular battle: 110,000 of Archelaus's army were killed, and barely 10,000 are said to have survived. On learning of the disaster Mithridates sent from Asia 70,000 of his choicest soldiers to help Archelaus. In the second battle 50,000 of these were killed, and there Diogenes, son of Archelaus, was butchered. In the third battle all the forces Archelaus had were wiped out. For 20,000 of his soldiers, driven into a marsh, when they begged for Sulla's protection, were killed by the insatiable anger of the victor, and as many others were forced into a river and drowned, and the rest of the wretches were butchered here and there. Besides, Mithridates had set his mind on killing the chief men of the noblest cities in Asia and confiscating their goods. And when he had already killed 1,600 in this way, the Ephesians, fearing the example, shut out his garrison and barred their gates; the people of Smyrna, Sardis, Colophon and Tralles did the same. Mithridates, alarmed, made terms of peace with Sulla through his general Archelaus. Meanwhile Fimbria, a henchman of the Marian crimes and the boldest man of all, killed at Nicomedia the consul Flaccus, to whom he had gone as legate; and soon, taking over the army, he chased the son of Mithridates out of Asia to Miletopolis, attacked the king's station, drove the king himself from Pergamum, pursued him as he fled, and besieged him at Pitane; and he would surely have taken him, had L. Lucullus put the care of the commonwealth before civil quarrels and been willing to hem him in by sea by placing his fleet in the way. Then Fimbria, angry with the people of Ilium, by whom he believed he had been kept out by the barring of their gates, out of their support for Sulla's party, utterly destroyed with slaughter and fire the city of Ilium itself, that ancient parent of Rome. But Sulla at once rebuilt it. The same Fimbria, when he was besieged at Thyatira by Sulla's army, driven by despair, killed himself with his own hand in the temple of Aesculapius. Fannius and Magius, deserters from Fimbria's army, joined Mithridates: at their urging Mithridates made a treaty with Sertorius through envoys sent into Spain. Sertorius sent M. Marius to him to confirm the treaty: the king kept him with him and soon made him general in place of Archelaus, who had gone over to Sulla with his wife and children. Marius and Eumachus, generals sent by Mithridates against Lucullus, soon gathered a great army, joined battle with P. Rutilius at Chalcedon, and cut him down with the greatest part of his army. Lucullus surrounded Mithridates with a ditch as he besieged Cyzicus and forced him to suffer what he was doing; and he sent a messenger to the Cyzicenes themselves, to bid them be of good heart, one of his soldiers skilled at swimming, who, held up on two wineskins, holding the crossbar in the middle himself and rowing with his feet beneath, swam across seven miles. Mithridates, labouring under want, ordered part of his forces, equipped with arms, to go home: Lucullus caught it and destroyed it all, for he is said to have killed more than 15,000 men then. Then too Fannius, who had joined Mithridates, and Metrophanes, the king's praetor, defeated by Mamercus, fled with 2,000 horsemen into Moesia, and turning from there into Maeonia, fell upon the hills and the Inarime plains. There not only are scorched mountains and rocks seen, darkened as if by a kind of soot, but the plains too, their soil burned through, lie rough and crumbling over fifty miles without a single sign of fire or furnace, with ash hanging down into the depths; in three places too burning chasms are shown, which the Greeks call physae. Wandering among them for a long time, they were at last saved from unexpected dangers and came secretly into the king's camp. Deiotarus, king of Gallograecia, butchered the king's prefects in war. Meanwhile Mithridates at Cyzicus, besieged by the same delay by which he was besieging, forced his army into great want and pestilence. For he is said to have lost more than 300,000 men by hunger and sickness in that same siege; he himself took a ship and fled secretly from the camp with a few. Lucullus, a spectator of another's disaster with his soldiers unbloodied, won a new kind of victory; soon he attacked Marius, defeated him and put him to flight: in this battle more than 11,000 of Marius's soldiers are said to have been killed. Lucullus afterwards joined battle with the same Marius at sea and either sank or took thirty-two of the king's ships and many transports. Many of those whom Sulla had proscribed were slain there. Marius, dragged out the next day from the cave where he was hiding, paid the deserved penalty of his hostile spirit. With the same onset Lucullus laid waste Apamea, and under Mount Olympus he took and stormed Prusa, a most strongly fortified city, and plundered it. Mithridates, sailing against Byzantium with a fleet equipped, was caught by a storm and lost eighty beaked ships; when he himself was sinking in a ship already shattered, he leapt across into the light galley of Seleucus the pirate, the pirate himself helping him. From there he reached Sinope, and afterwards Amisus, with great difficulty.

## 6.3

In the same year at Rome Catiline was accused of unchastity, because he was charged with having committed it with the Vestal virgin Fabia, and he escaped, propped up by the favour of Catulus. Lucullus had besieged Sinope to storm it; Seleucus the arch-pirate and Cleochares the eunuch, who were in command there as its garrison, plundered it, set it on fire and left it. Lucullus, moved by the internal disaster of his wretched enemies, by a swift arrival put out the fire that had been set. So the wretched city, with the roles of enemies and allies reversed, was destroyed by those who should have defended it and saved by those who should have destroyed it. But M. Lucullus, who had succeeded Curio in Macedonia, attacked the whole people of the Bessi in war and received them in surrender. At the same time Metellus, praetor of Sicily, had found Sicily afflicted by that most foul praetorship of C. Verres, and torn above all by the wicked plunder and slaughter of Pyrganio the arch-pirate, who, having driven off the Roman fleet, had taken the harbour of Syracuse; Metellus soon crushed him in a battle by sea and land and forced him to leave Sicily. Besides, Lucullus crossed the Euphrates and the Tigris, joined battle with Mithridates and Tigranes at the city of Tigranocerta, and with a very small band of his own killed a great number of the enemy; for 30,000 men are said to have been cut down in that war. Tigranes fled, attended by barely 150 horsemen, having thrown away his diadem and tiara so as not to be recognised. Then suppliant envoys from almost the whole East came to Lucullus; with winter coming on he went back through Armenia into Mesopotamia and stormed and took the city of Nisibis, then famous in those parts.

## 6.4

In the same days pirates, scattered over all the seas, and no longer only cutting off the supply ships but laying waste islands and provinces too, were growing beyond measure, as men commonly joined them for the impunity of crime and greed for plunder: Cn. Pompeius crushed them with wonderful speed, though after much laying waste, which they had long carried on by land and sea. At the same time Metellus overthrew the island of Crete over two years, brought it under control, tamed by a long war, and exchanged the laws of Minos for Roman laws. Afterwards Pompeius, successor of Lucullus, shut in the king's camp by siege in Lesser Armenia near Mount Dastracus. The king made a sally by night with all his forces and resolved also to drive back his pursuer in battle. Pompeius set himself to pursue them as they fled. So battle was joined by night. The moon had then risen behind the Romans. The king's men, taking the length of the shadows for the nearness of the enemy, poured out all their missiles in vain. The Romans afterwards attacked them as if unarmed and won without labour. For 40,000 of the king's army were cut down or taken; of the Romans a thousand were wounded and barely forty killed. The king slipped away in flight amid the confusion of battle and escaped, helped too by the kindness of the half-lit night; and abandoned by all his friends, philosophers, writers of history and of poems, and physicians, alone, leading his horse by hand through out-of-the-way places and trembling at every noise of the night, he turned aside to a certain fort and from there went on into Armenia. Pompeius, about to pursue the king, founded the city of Nicopolis, with old men, camp followers and the sick who were willing, between two rivers that rise from one mountain from different caves, that is the Euphrates and the Araxes. He pardoned Tigranes when he begged; he defeated three times in battle the army of Horodes, king of the Albani, and his prefects; afterwards he gladly accepted letters and gifts from Horodes for restoring peace with the Albani; he routed Artaces, king of Hiberia, in war and received all Hiberia in surrender. From there, when he had settled Armenia, the Colchians, Cappadocia and Syria, setting their affairs in order, he moved on from Pontus into Parthia and came on the fiftieth day to the city of Ecbatana, the capital of the Parthian kingdom.

## 6.5

In the Bosporus, as Mithridates was celebrating the rites of Ceres, an earthquake so heavy suddenly arose that great destruction of cities and fields is said to have followed from it. At the same time Castor, Mithridates's prefect, who commanded at Phanagoria, killed the king's friends, seized the citadel, and sent four sons of Mithridates over to the Roman garrisons. Mithridates, inflamed with anger, blazed out into crimes. For he then killed many of his friends and his son Exipodras, when he had already before butchered Machares in another murder of kin; Pharnaces, another of his sons, frightened by the example of his brothers, won over to himself the army sent to pursue him and soon led it against his father. Mithridates long begged his son in vain from the highest wall, and when he saw him unmoved, about to die, he is said to have cried out: "Since Pharnaces orders me to die, you, gods of my fathers, if you exist, I pray that one day he too may hear this cry from his own children." And at once, going down to his wives, concubines and daughters, he gave poison to them all. When he himself had drunk it last, and yet could not be overcome by the poison because of the remedies with which he had often walled up his vital parts against harmful juices, and was walking about to no purpose, to see if by some means the poison poured in might at last run through his veins driven by the stirring of the body, he called on a certain Gallic soldier who was running about, the wall now broken, and offered him his throat. This was the end of life that Mithridates had, and he left us a very strong proof of his opinion, a man, as they say, the most superstitious of all, seventy-two years old, always having with him philosophers and men most skilled in all the arts. "If you exist," he said, "gods of my fathers." So he, by long worshipping and long seeking, had come to feel that these were not certain gods who were thought to be. A king of much experience and heavy with age, he did not understand the true God, to the knowledge of whom one comes only by hearing, through faith. But that these were false he had seen through by the light of reason itself, giving one thing to custom and another to his own mind. "If you exist, gods," he said, which is to say: I, feeling that there is above man a power more mighty than man himself, moved by the need to pray, commend my diligence and excuse my ignorance; I call on Him who is, while I address those who are not. So it is to be considered with grief and fear: of what punishment or of what judgement will they be worthy who, against the prohibition of the truth now made plain and public, follow and worship those gods of whom even those men could already then doubt who as yet could know nothing besides them? Yet I ask briefly: what kind of times did they seem then to the whole East, when for forty years wretched nations were worn down by the alternate laying waste of such great generals; when every city, in the middle of such great clashes, was inevitably in danger, bound to inflame one side by the means by which it had calmed the other, and soon to have as punishment what it had had for a time as remedy; when the trembling embassies of different provinces were shifted between the Roman generals as they followed one after another and Mithridates, more savage through acquaintance, going to each in turn, as the fortune of war raised up each, and with uncertain satisfactions, increasing the dangers they were curing? For what Pompeius, and Pompeius the most moderate man among the Romans, did at once through very many parts of the East when the Mithridatic war was finished, I shall set out in a few words.

## 6.6

In the 689th year from the founding of the city, in the consulship of M. Tullius Cicero and C. Antonius, Pompeius, having received the news that Mithridates had been killed, attacked in war Coele-Syria and Phoenicia, first tamed the Ituraeans and the Arabs and took their city, which they call Petra; from there he sent Gabinius with an army to the Jews, over whom Aristobulus, having driven out his brother Hyrcanus, was the first to rule as king after being priest, and to Jerusalem their city. He himself at once followed, and was received into the city by the fathers but driven back by the common people from the wall of the temple, and he set himself to storm it. It was fortified not only by the nature of the place but also by a huge wall and a very great ditch, and though he forced legions to take turns one after another day and night without rest, he barely stormed it in the third month. 13,000 Jews are said to have been cut down there; the rest of the multitude came into his protection. Pompeius ordered the walls of the city to be overthrown and levelled with the ground, and, when he had struck some of the chief men of the Jews with the axe, he restored Hyrcanus to the priesthood and led Aristobulus captive to Rome. Pompeius himself told before an assembly that he had waged this war of the East with twenty-two kings. Meanwhile the conspiracy of Catiline against his country was formed and betrayed in the city in those same days, and in Etruria put out in a civil war; at Rome those privy to the conspiracy were killed. But it is enough that this history, well enough known to all, with Cicero acting in it and Sallust describing it, has now been touched on briefly by us. A rising too that arose among the Paeligni, started by the Marcelli, father and son, was betrayed by L. Vettius, and when Catiline's conspiracy was laid open it was crushed as if its root had been cut; and both were punished, through Bibulus among the Paeligni and through Cicero among the Bruttii.

## 6.7

In the 693rd year from the founding of the city, in the consulship of C. Caesar and L. Bibulus, by the Vatinian law three provinces with seven legions were given to Caesar for five years: Transalpine and Cisalpine Gaul and Illyricum; the senate afterwards added Long-haired Gaul. Suetonius Tranquillus has set out this history most fully, from whom we have picked suitable small portions. A certain Orgetorix, a chief of the people, had inflamed to arms, with the hope of invading all the Gauls, the spirits of the Helvetii, the bravest people of all the Gauls, above all for this reason, that they were wrangling in almost continual war with the Germans, from whom they are separated only by the river Rhine. When he was seized by the other nobles and forced to his death, they still could not hold back the common people, once roused for plunder. These, having made a conspiracy and set a day, burned their villages and houses, so that there should be no longing from the hope of returning, and set out. When Caesar had met them at the river Rhone, he defeated them twice in a great and difficult war and forced them, defeated, to surrender. When it first set out, the whole multitude of these, the Helvetii, Tulingi, Latobogii, Rauraci and Boii, of both sexes, was up to 157,000 people. Of these 47,000 fell in the war; the rest were sent back to their own lands. Afterwards Caesar defeated among the Sequani King Ariovistus, who was stirring up and bringing with him incredible forces of Germans, with which he boasted that he had lately subdued all the peoples of the Gauls; this after Caesar's army, terrified by the number and valour of the Germans, had long refused battle. At once Ariovistus fled into Germany, taking a small boat and crossing the Rhine; his two wives and as many daughters were taken. In Ariovistus's army there were the Harudes, Marcomanni, Triboci, Vangiones, Nemetes, Sedusii and Suebi. The battle was very heavy above all because of the phalanx of the Germans, which they had built up, safe on every side, to break the Roman line, gathering into one column and joining their shields together above their heads. But after some of the Roman soldiers, notable for nimbleness and boldness, leapt up onto the covering tortoise, tore off the shields one by one like scales, and stabbed from above the bare shoulders of those caught and uncovered, the enemy, terrified by the new danger of death, broke up the terrible structure. Then, turned to flight, they were cut down insatiably over fifty miles, and the number of the Germans could not be guessed, either how great it was that was present at the battle or how great that of the slain. After this the people of the Belgae, who are the third part of the Gauls, blazed up against Caesar: and their forces, part by part, were these. The Bellovaci, who seemed to excel the rest in number and valour, had 60,000 choice armed men; the Suessiones from twelve towns 50,000; the Nervii, whose ferocity was proclaimed to be so untamed that until that time they had never let merchants come to them to bring wine and other wares, by whose pleasure, once brought in, sluggishness would be brought to their valour, had likewise 50,000; the Atrebates too and the Ambiani 10,000, the Morini 25,000, the Menapii 9,000, the Caleti 10,000, the Veliocasses and Viromandui likewise 10,000, the Atuatuci 18,000; the Condrusi, Eburones, Caerosi and Caemani, who by one name are called Germans, 40,000. So there are said to have been 272,000 choice armed men. As they burst suddenly out of a wood, Caesar's army was thrown into confusion and driven to flight, losing very many of its men; at last, at the urging of its general, it stood, attacked the victors and destroyed them almost to extermination.

## 6.8

So when Caesar, having done great things in Gaul, had decided to set out for Italy, he sent Galba with the twelfth legion to the Veragri and Seduni. When he had settled to winter in a village of the Veragri whose name was Octodurus, and had granted to the inhabitants the middle part of the town, which was divided by a torrent, one day he saw that they had gone away in the night and settled on the nearest hill. For they, despising the small number of barely half a legion, thought that a spoil offering itself would fall to them with no trouble, and had called their neighbours into this partnership of slaughter and plunder. So while Galba was hemmed in and alarmed by such present dangers, and among various consultations uncertain of a sure plan, suddenly the Gauls, pouring down the mountain, surrounded the unfinished camp and loaded the defenders, few along the rampart, with stones and missiles. And when the camp was already being broken into, on the advice of Pacuvius, a chief centurion, and Volusenus, a tribune, all the Romans burst out of the gates, and attacking the enemy suddenly, off their guard, first threw them into confusion and then routed them, turned to flight, with pitiful slaughter. For more than 30,000 barbarians are said to have been cut down then. So when Caesar thought that all the peoples of the Gauls were now pacified, he was drawn back into a new and very great war. For while the young P. Crassus was wintering with the seventh legion among the Andecavi, by the Ocean, the Veneti and the others bordering on them suddenly conspired in arms, put the Roman envoys in chains, and told the Romans that they would give them back only if they got back their own hostages. As allies for this war they took on the Osismi, Lexovii, Namnetes, Ambiliati, Morini, Diablintes and Menapii; they also sent for help from Britain. Caesar, informed by Crassus of the rebellion of the surrendered peoples, though he understood how great the difficulty of entering on the war was, yet thought that a matter of such importance must not be neglected, so that the licence to dare such things should not be loosened for the rest by the example; and so, having tried in vain to pursue the enemy by battle on land (since the enemy were protected by the estuaries running in from the Ocean and by inaccessible retreats in safe bays of the land), he ordered warships to be built on the river Loire: when, brought down by it into the Ocean, they were seen by the enemy, at once 220 of their ships, ready and very well equipped with every kind of arms, came out of the harbour and took up position opposite. When Brutus saw that the fight of the ships was far unequal, because the ships of the barbarians, built of solid oak and hardened with very strong ribs, blunted like rocks the blows of the beaked ships driven against them, it was a help first of all that he had prepared very sharp sickles, not fixed firmly to poles but tied on with ropes, with which, when it was needed, they would catch the rigging from a distance and, drawing back the sickle by the rope after the shafts had been pulled away, cut it. When these had quickly been made ready, he ordered the tackle of the enemy's yards to be broken: so, as the yards fell, he at once made many ships motionless, as if taken. Others, terrified by this danger, tried to flee with sails set wherever the wind drove, and soon, left helpless as the wind dropped, were a sport for the Romans; and so, when all the ships had been burned and the Gauls who had fought had been killed, all the rest surrendered. But Caesar, above all because of the wrong done to the envoys, and to brand a mark of terrible example on a people fickle in all its counsels, killed all the chief men by tortures and sold the rest as slaves. In the same days Titurius Sabinus, making a sally, destroyed with unbelievable slaughter the Aulerci, Eburovices and Lexovii, who had killed their own nobles because they were unwilling to be authors of reviving the war. Publius Crassus, when he had reached Aquitania, was met by war. For the Sontiates, attacking the Romans with a great cavalry and very strong forces of foot, long threw them into heavy confusion; afterwards, defeated and forced into the town of the Sontiates and besieged, when they saw that they would be stormed, they handed over their arms and were received in surrender. The Aquitani, moved by the disaster, gathered an army from every side; they also sent for help from Nearer Spain; they put in command of the war above all those who had served with Sertorius. All of these, while they were preparing a siege for Crassus, were destroyed in their own camp, Crassus overwhelming them. For of the Aquitani and Cantabri, 50,000 of whom had then come to help, 38,000 are said to have been cut down. Caesar attacked in war the Germans, who had crossed the Rhine with immense forces and were preparing to subject all the Gauls to themselves at the same time, and cut them down to extermination. Their number, they say, was up to 440,000.

## 6.9

Then Caesar made a bridge and crossed into Germany, freed the Sugambri and Ubii from siege, and terrified by his coming the Suebi, a very great and most ferocious people, who, as many have reported, have a hundred cantons and peoples, and the whole of Germany; soon, breaking down the bridge, he withdrew into Gaul. From there he came to the Morini, from whom the crossing into Britain is nearest and shortest. Having prepared about eighty transport ships and light vessels, he crossed into Britain. There he was first worn out by a bitter battle, then caught by a contrary storm, and lost the greatest part of his fleet and no small number of soldiers, and almost all of his cavalry. Going back into Gaul, he sent the legions into winter quarters and ordered six hundred ships of both kinds to be made. When he had crossed again into Britain with these at the beginning of spring, while he himself went on against the enemy with the army, the ships standing at anchor, caught by a storm, were either dashed against each other or driven onto the sands and broken up: of these forty perished, and the rest were repaired with great difficulty. Caesar's cavalry was defeated by the Britons at the first encounter, and there the tribune Labienus was killed. In a second battle he turned the defeated Britons to flight, with great danger to his own men. From there he set out to the river Thames, which they say can be crossed by fords in only one place. On its farther bank an immense multitude of the enemy had settled under the leader Cassivellaunus, and had fronted the bank of the river and almost the whole ford under water with very sharp stakes. When this was discovered and avoided by the Romans, the barbarians, unable to bear the charge of the legions, hid themselves in the woods, from which they tore at the Romans heavily and often with frequent sallies. Meanwhile the strongest state of the Trinobantes, with its leader Mandubracius, gave forty hostages and surrendered to Caesar. Following this example, several other cities came into treaty with the Romans, and with them pointing the way Caesar at last, after a heavy fight, took the town of Cassivellaunus, set between two marshes, fortified besides by the cover of woods, and very full of everything.

## 6.10

Then Caesar, having returned from the Britons into Gaul, after he had sent the legions into winter quarters, was surrounded and harried on every side by sudden uproars of war. For Ambiorix, conspiring with the Eburones and Atuatuci, encouraged by the counsel of the Treveri, surrounded in an ambush among the Eburones the legates Cotta and Sabinus and killed them with their whole legion, utterly. Ambiorix, elated by this victory, hastily gathered to arms the Atuatuci and Nervii and very many others, and hurried against the legate Cicero, who likewise was then in command of a legion in winter quarters. The multitude of the enemy could be gathered from this: when, in the siege of the camp, they were taught by Roman captives that a rampart must be put round it, and they did not have farm tools, by cutting the earth with their swords and carrying it out in their cloaks they completed in barely three hours a rampart of ten feet and a ditch of fifteen feet over fifteen miles in circuit. Besides, they built a hundred and twenty towers of wonderful height. And when the wedges of the enemy, relieving one another, had now fought for seven days and nights, and a very strong wind had suddenly arisen, they hurled red-hot potsherds with slings and threw darts set alight in fires, and soon glowing with the fire they had caught, into the camp. When this was done, the wind, pressing hard through the straw roofs, fanned the scattered fire. But not even so did the Romans, though overwhelmed on every side, give way to wounds, toils, watches, fasting or fires. At last it was reported to Caesar that one legion had been destroyed and the second was already almost finished. As Caesar approached with two legions, the enemy left the siege and all rushed against him, snatching up their forces. Caesar purposely hid himself in a very small camp, and, sending the horsemen ahead, ordered them to feign flight, so as to entice the enemy, in contempt of him, to cross the valley that lay between, which seemed dangerous to himself. As they came on, he ordered the gates to be blocked besides. On seeing this the Gauls, as if they had already won, turned to surrounding the rampart from outside. Caesar suddenly poured out his army, ready, from all the gates, and finished off the Gauls, turned to flight, with most vast slaughter. For there are said to have been 60,000 of them then, of whom a few escaped through pathless marshes. Indutiomarus, chief of the Treveri, who had great forces of armed men, after he had been informed of the agreement of all Gaul, resolved to destroy the camp of Labienus and the legion he commanded, which he thought easy to do, and then, joined with the Eburones and Nervii, to go on to crush Caesar. Labienus, by whatever arts he could, feigned fear, and so, when Indutiomarus was wandering rather carelessly before the rampart with his forces jeering, struck him down by a sudden sally. By this victory of Labienus the remaining attempts of the Gauls were checked, and Caesar was a little quieter for the rest of the winter. But, understanding that greater business of war remained for him, above all because, having lost the greatest part of his army and with others heavily wounded, he did not seem fit even to withstand, not to say to crush, the onslaught of the Gauls, he asked the proconsul Cn. Pompeius to enrol legions and send them to help him: and so before the winter was out three legions came to him in camp. So Caesar, before the enemy's forces could join into one, prepared at the beginning of spring to attack them while alarmed and crush them while scattered in their own territories. So first he plundered the territory of the Nervii, and gave the spoil, which was very plentiful, to the army. Then he invaded with three columns the Menapii, who thought themselves very well protected by their immense marshes and most tangled woods, and after excessive slaughter had been carried on everywhere he received the rest, as suppliants, in surrender. Labienus in the following battle killed all the forces of the Treveri, drawn into battle by a trick before they could join the Germans who were coming up, and at once took the city itself. Caesar, wishing to avenge the death of the legates Sabinus and Cotta, after he learned that Ambiorix and the Eburones, the authors of the destruction of the legion, had fled back into the Ardennes forest (which forest is the greatest of all Gaul and reaches from the banks of the Rhine and the territory of the Treveri as far as the Nervii, and stretches in length more than fifty miles), measuring that it would be a matter of the greatest danger for his men if they were divided, as strangers, through blocked and spacious woods and looked for an enemy who knew the places best, invited all Gaul by messengers to seek out and plunder, each as he pleased, the spoils hidden in the Ardennes forest. When this was done, with Gauls dying on both sides, he avenged the very great wrongs done to the Romans without danger to any Roman. So, secure by this safest kind of victory, he returned into Italy.

## 6.11

So when Caesar had returned into Italy, Gaul again conspired in arms, and many peoples came together at once. Their leader was Vercingetorix, on whose advice all the Gauls at once of their own accord set fire to their own cities: the first to be burned by its own people was Bituriges. From there they made an attack on Caesar, who had run back secretly by forced marches through the Narbonese province to the army. Caesar had then shut in by siege a town called Cenabum: after it had long been attacked, at last, after many disasters for the Romans, on a rainy day, when the cords and sinews of the enemy's engines had grown slack, it was taken with towers brought up and destroyed. There are said to have been 40,000 men there: of whom barely eighty slipped away by flight and came to the nearest camp of the Gauls. Besides, the Arverni and the others bordering on them, having drawn the Aedui too to their side, made war against Caesar in many battles. When they, worn out by fighting, had withdrawn into a certain fortress, the soldiers, gaping after plunder, set their minds on storming the town, Caesar pleading in vain the unfavourableness of the place. So there Caesar was pressed by the enemy bursting out from above, lost a great part of his army, and fled defeated. While these things were being done at Alesia, Vercingetorix, whom all with equal agreement had chosen king, urged that all from the whole of Gaul who could bear arms should be ready for this war. For this was the one war from which there would follow either perpetual liberty or eternal slavery or the death of all. And so, apart from the number, beyond counting, that he had gathered before, about 8,000 horse and 250,000 foot were gathered. Then the Romans and Gauls took two hills facing each other. From there, fighting often with many sallies and with various outcomes, at last the Romans won by the outstanding valour of the German horsemen, long their friends, whom they had now called in to help. Vercingetorix, on another day, having gathered all who had escaped by flight, said that he had in good faith been the author of defending liberty and breaking the treaty, and that now he would be ready in spirit, whether they all offered themselves to the Romans for death or gave him up alone for all. So the Gauls, taking up as if on the king's advice the wish that they had covered for some time out of shame, at once begged pardon for themselves and handed him over alone as the author of a great crime. The Bellovaci were held, in their own opinion, braver than all the peoples of the Gauls. These, under the leader Correus, renewed the war, joined to themselves in this partnership of the war they had taken up the Ambiani, Aulerci, Caleti, Veliocasses and Atrebates, and took a certain place surrounded and hemmed in on every side by marshes; and when battle was joined they butchered a great band of the Remi, who were helping the Romans. Then, when they themselves had occupied a place suitable for an ambush, chosen beforehand, and the Romans, learning of this, had come to the place of the ambush drawn up and in order, when battle was joined the Romans shut in the fleeing Gauls by the same defences of the place by which they had been enclosed, and cut them all down to extermination. There Correus, refusing either flight or surrender, killing the Romans who were pressing to take him alive, forced them to kill him. So when Caesar thought that all Gaul was pacified and would not dare to aim at any risings, he sent the legions into winter quarters; he himself, however, laid waste with horrible slaughter of men the territory of Ambiorix, who had stirred up so many wars. But the legate C. Caninius found war among the Pictones: there a great multitude of the enemy surrounded a legion hampered on the march and brought it into the last danger. Then the legate Fabius, having received Caninius's letter, set out for the Pictones, and there, informed by captives about the lie of the land, he crushed the enemy unawares, and, having made great slaughter, drove off very much spoil. Then, when he had given Caninius a signal of his arrival, Caninius suddenly leapt out with his whole camp and threw himself on the enemy. So, with Fabius pressing from one side and Caninius from the other, in a very great and long battle countless forces of the Gauls were butchered. From there Fabius set out against the Carnutes; for he knew that Dumnacus, their leader, the most long-standing inciter of the whole rebellion, who had slipped away from this war, would again stir up very great uproars in Gaul if he joined the Aremorican peoples. But he tamed them, while they were still alarmed by the very newness, with wonderful valour and speed. Meanwhile Drappes, and Lucterius together with him, when they saw that Caninius and the legions were in their territory, gathered forces from every side and seized the town of Uxellodunum. This town hung on the highest citadel of a mountain; on two sides it was surrounded by a river of no small size along steep slopes; then, secure with a very ample spring halfway down, and safe with a very great supply of corn within, it looked down on the vain runnings about of the enemy far off. Caninius, as only Roman foresight could, called out both leaders onto the plain with the greatest part of their forces and overcame them in a very great battle. For when one of the leaders was killed the other fled with very few, and none returned to the town. But to storm it Caesar was needed. So, informed by messengers, Caesar hurried up, and having looked about at everything he saw that, if he tried to storm it by force, his army would be destroyed for the sport and show of the enemy; that there was one single safeguard, if somehow the enemy could be kept from water. But even this no one but Caesar could have done, since the spring that they used for drinking poured out halfway down the sloping side of the mountain. Caesar ordered mantlets to be moved up to the nearest point to the spring and a tower to be built. At once there was a great rush out of the town. Though the Romans resisted stubbornly and came up more and more often, very many were butchered by them, as they fought without danger. So a mound was built and a tower of sixty feet, whose top could be level with the place of the spring, so that missiles could be thrown on equal terms and rolling masses of rocks hurled from above need not be feared. But the townsmen, when they saw that not only their cattle but also the weaker ages of men were dying of thirst, filled barrels with pitch, tallow and shingles, then set them on fire and hurled them down the slope, and followed them themselves, pouring out from the whole town. When Caesar saw that, with his engines burning, the battle was heavy and dangerous for his men, he ordered cohorts to go swiftly round the town by a hidden way and suddenly raise a vast shout on every side. When this was done, the townsmen, alarmed, wishing to run back to defend the town, drew back from attacking the tower and pulling down the mound. Yet those who, safe under cover of the mound, were digging tunnels to cut the veins of the spring, found the channels of the waters in their hidden place and, by dividing them into many, made them grow thin and use themselves up. The townsmen, when the spring dried up, seized by final despair, surrendered. But Caesar cut off the hands of all who had borne arms and left them their lives, so that the punishment of the wicked might be better witnessed even to later generations. For a set example of punishment counts for much in checking boldness, when the very present shape of a wretched man living both warns those privy to it to remember and forces those who do not know to ask.

## 6.12

When the Gauls had been drained and tamed, Caesar returned secure with the legions into Italy, dreading no risings of the Gauls behind him, knowing for certain that he had left hardly any who would either dare to rise or, if they rose, were to be feared. I should like now to set before our eyes Gaul, bloodless and exhausted, after those most burning fevers and the inner heats that scorch the better parts of the vitals: how she is, with how great leanness and how great pallor, how downcast and loosened she lies, how she dreads even the movements of necessary duty, lest they call back the same onset of evils. For the Roman army burst on her with a sudden onslaught, like a stronger plague on a very strong body, which is inflamed the more heavily the more impatiently it is borne. She thirsted, wretched as she was, when, with the sword pressing on her, she was forced to declare a pledge of eternal slavery, her hostages besides torn away; she thirsted, as I said, for that well-known sweetness of liberty, the sweetest of all to all, as if for cold water, and the more she understood that it was being taken from her, the more greedily she longed for it. Hence that so frequent presumption against what was forbidden: an untimely liberty was seized upon to defend liberty, and the licence of possessing, snatched insatiably, increased the ruin that it seemed to put out, though the ruin was wrongly conceived. Hence the Roman was a more cunning plotter before battle, hence a more hostile enemy in battle, hence a harsher victor after battle; hence everything grew more cruel to tame impatience; hence there was no longer trust even in remedies. So if I could ask this nation of which we are speaking what it judged of those times then, when it was bearing these things, it would answer, I think, saying: "That fever then made me so bloodless and so cold that even this, which has grazed almost everyone, could not heat or stir me; and the Romans so bowed me down that I do not rise even against the Goths." But not even Rome herself escaped the disasters she brought on others. The powers of generals and the strength of legions were long exercised and increased through all the hinges of the world, to clash with one another and conquer to her loss, at whose peril they would be conquered. For civil wars came along with Caesar as he returned victorious from Gaul, and other very heavy evils went before: the killing of Crassus among the Parthians and the butchering of his army.

## 6.13

In the 697th year from the founding of the city Crassus, colleague of Pompeius in the consulship, having drawn by lot the province against the Parthians, a man of insatiable greed, hearing of the wealth of the temple at Jerusalem, which Pompeius had left untouched, turned aside into Palestine, went to Jerusalem, broke into the temple and plundered its wealth. From there, making for Parthia through Mesopotamia, wherever he went he imposed levies on the allied cities and exacted money, and as soon as he had crossed the Euphrates, he at once met Vagises, an envoy sent to him by Horodes, king of the Parthians, by whom he was fiercely rebuked for having crossed the Euphrates against the treaty of Lucullus and Pompeius, led on by greed. Therefore, the envoy said, it would come about without delay that he would be loaded with Seric iron instead of Parthian gold. So when they had come near Carrhae, the Parthians, suddenly falling on them with the prefects Surena and Silaces, crushed the Romans with arrows. Very many senators fell there, some men of consular and praetorian rank too; Crassus also, son of Crassus, a most choice young man, was killed in the line. Besides, four cohorts with the legate Vargunteius were caught in the middle of the plains and killed. Surena, taking his cavalry, set himself to pursue Crassus, surrounded him and killed him as he vainly sought a parley, although he would rather have carried him off alive. A few, freed by the kindness of the night, fled to Carrhae. When the disaster of the Romans was known, many provinces of the East would have fallen away from their alliance with and loyalty to the Roman people, had not Cassius, gathering a few soldiers from the rout, held down a swelling Syria with outstanding courage of mind and moderation; he both defeated and killed in battle Antiochus and his huge forces, and also drove out in war the Parthians sent by Horodes into Syria, who had already entered Antioch, and killed their leader Osaces.

## 6.14

So the Roman state is always driven by alternate change, like the shape of the Ocean sea, which, unlike every day, now rises with increases following one another for seven days, now in the same number of following days is drawn off by a natural loss and an inner ebb. For to begin from the nearest: when, with the Cimbri and Tigurini winning, the Roman army perished at the river Rhone, Rome felt the tightest straits; when the disaster was at once turned back on the Cimbri, elated by great advances, she forgot her earlier ebbs. Next the Italian war and Sulla's tearing apart chastised this boasting of most recent prosperity. Again, after this domestic and internal ruin, by which she was disembowelled and eaten away almost to the marrow, in almost equal spaces of time she was not only restored but even extended, when Lucullus tamed Asia, Pompeius Spain and Caesar Gaul, and the Roman Empire was spread almost to the farthest bounds of the earth. This most ample enlargement is now followed by a most vast ruin. For among the Parthians a Roman consul is killed and an army destroyed, that most atrocious civil war of Pompeius and Caesar is joined, and amid these things Rome herself, seized by a sudden fire, is burned up. For in the 700th year from the founding of the city a fire, gathered no one knows from where, invaded the greatest part of the city, and they say that never before was the city seized and laid waste by so great a fire. For it is handed down to memory that fourteen districts were consumed, together with the Vicus Jugarius. From here the civil war is joined, which had long been prepared by great quarrels and schemes.

## 6.15

For Caesar, returning victorious from Gaul, demanded that a second consulship be decreed to him in his absence. This was opposed by the consul Marcellus, with Pompeius supporting, and then it was decreed by the senate that Caesar should not come into the city unless he had dismissed his army; and by the authority of the consul Marcellus, Pompeius was sent with command to the legions that were at Luceria. Caesar went to Ravenna. M. Antonius and P. Cassius, tribunes of the plebs, interceding for Caesar, were barred from the senate house and the forum by the prohibition of the consul Lentulus, and set out to Caesar, with Curio and Caelius going with them. Caesar, having crossed the river Rubicon, as soon as he came to Ariminum, told the five cohorts that he then had, his only ones, with which, as Livy says, he attacked the world, what needed to be done. Deploring the wrongs done to him, he declared that the cause of the civil war was the restoring of the tribunes to their country. Then through Antonius he received from Lucretius seven cohorts that were stationed at Sulmo, and brought over to his side three legions that were stationed with Domitius at Corfinium. Pompeius and the whole senate, alarmed as Caesar's strength grew, crossed over into Greece as if driven from Italy, and chose Dyrrachium as the seat for waging the war. Caesar came to Rome, broke down the doors and seized the money from the treasury that had been refused him, and brought out of it 4,135 pounds of gold and almost 900,000 pounds of silver. Going from there to the legions at Ariminum, he soon crossed the Alps and came to Massilia; leaving Trebonius with three legions to besiege it, because he had not been received, he hurried to the Spains, which L. Afranius, M. Petreius and M. Varro, Pompeius's generals, were holding with legions. There he overcame Petreius and Afranius in many battles and let them go on agreed terms. In Further Spain he took over two legions from M. Varro. Likewise his generals too: Curio drove Cato out of Sicily, Valerius drove Cotta out of Sardinia, and Varus threw Tubero out of Africa. Caesar, returning to Massilia, tamed it by siege, granted it only life and liberty, and scraped away everything else. But Dolabella, of Caesar's party, defeated in Illyricum by Octavius and Libo and stripped of his forces, fled to Antonius. Basilus and Sallust, each with the one legion he commanded, and likewise Antonius, and Hortensius too, hurrying up with a fleet from the Lower Sea, all set out together against Octavius and Libo and were defeated. When Antonius had surrendered to Octavius with fifteen cohorts, all were led off by Libo to Pompeius. Curio crossed with an army from Sicily into Africa: King Juba at once caught him and butchered him with all his forces. Octavius, trying to storm Salonae, lost almost all the forces he led. Caelius deserted Caesar and joined Milo, the exile; and when both tried to storm Capua with a band of slaves, they were killed. Bibulus, at Corcyra, overcome by shame because the enemy had made a mockery of the watch he kept spread over the sea and the town, wore himself out by fasting and sleeplessness. Appius Claudius Censorinus, who was guarding Greece by Pompeius's order, wished to test the credit of the Pythian oracle, which had already been done away with: for it is said that the prophetess, forced by him to go down into the cave, answered him as he consulted about the war: "This war, Roman, does not concern you; you will hold the Coela of Euboea." The Coela is what they call the Euboean gulf. So Appius went away uncertain at the tangled response. This consulter warns us to consult our detractors about something. They complain, surely, that by the faith of the Christians their rites have been forbidden them and their ceremonies taken away, and that above all for this reason, that, since entrails and prophecies have ceased, future disasters are not avoided, because they cannot be known. Why then, long before the rule of Caesar and the birth of Christ, as their own authors testify, had the credit of the Pythian oracle been done away with? And it was done away with because it was despised. And why despised, except because it was false or vain or doubtful? So the poet wisely warned beforehand: "Unanswered they go away, and hate the seat of the Sibyl." And lest they should perhaps think little of what was done away with and made obsolete by contempt, that is either the divinity or the seat: it was that Pythian Apollo who, they say, when the great serpent Python had been killed, the author and chief of all prophecy, came forward as heir of the seat, the divination and the name, and chose to give answers there, where divination itself seemed to have arisen with its author; whom, besides, in other parts of the earth too, all the madness of the frenzied belches out with foaming mouths and raging running about; to whom very many kings of the earth ran as if to the living voice of a consulted divinity; to whom the Romans themselves too very often sent the richest gifts. But if that Pythian Apollo was gradually despised, abandoned and done away with as experience made distinctions, what living thing can be hoped from a dead beast, what truth from a mad little woman? What, last, "when the fat Tyrrhenian has blown his ivory pipe at the altars", with the entrails of a fat beast laid out, will he not make up when hungry, if, as they themselves confess, Apollo himself leads astray by speaking obscure or false things? So let them bear with an even mind, even if they do not wish to imitate it meanwhile, that we forbid by the judgement of truth what their forefathers could despise even by experience. Meanwhile many kings of the East came with auxiliaries to Pompeius at Dyrrachium. When Caesar had come there, he surrounded Pompeius with a siege in vain, himself fronting the land with a ditch of fifteen miles, while the seas lay open to them. Pompeius overthrew a certain fort near the sea that Marcellinus was guarding and killed Caesar's garrisons that were stationed there. Caesar attacked Torquatus and one legion to storm it. When Pompeius learned of this danger to his allies, he gathered all his forces there: and Caesar at once left the siege and turned on him. But Torquatus, bursting out instantly, pursued him from behind. So Caesar's soldiers, terrified by danger on two sides, fled, Caesar himself trying in vain to stop them. But Pompeius, victor even by Caesar's testimony, called back his army as it pursued. 4,000 of Caesar's soldiers, twenty-two centurions and many Roman knights were cut down in that battle. Caesar then went on with a hurried march through Epirus into Thessaly; Pompeius followed with very great forces, and battle was joined. So a line was drawn up on both sides. Pompeius placed eighty-eight cohorts in triple order: there were 40,000 foot, six hundred horse on the left wing and five hundred on the right, besides many kings and very many senators and Roman knights, apart from a great force of light-armed troops. Caesar likewise drew up eighty cohorts in triple order; he had fewer than 30,000 foot and a thousand horse. There one might see and groan that the gathered strength of the Romans had taken its stand on the plains of Pharsalus for mutual slaughter, a strength which, had concord ruled it, no peoples, no kings could have withstood. At the first clash Pompeius's cavalry was driven back and left the left flank bare. Then, when they had long been cut down on both sides with doubtful fortune, and on the one side Pompeius, while exhorting, said "Spare the citizens" and yet did not do so, while on the other side Caesar did what he urged, saying "Soldier, strike the face", at last Pompeius's whole army fled and the camp was plundered. 15,000 of the Pompeians and thirty-three centurions were cut down in that battle. This was the outcome of the battle at Old Pharsalus. Pompeius, fleeing, found a transport ship at the mouth of the river Peneus and crossed into Asia. From there he came by Cyprus into Egypt, and there, as soon as he touched the shore, he was killed by order of the young Ptolemy, to win the favour of Caesar the victor. Pompeius's wife and sons fled; the rest of Pompeius's fleet was plundered, all who were in it being most cruelly butchered, and there Pompeius Bithynicus too was killed. Lentulus, a man of consular rank, was killed at Pelusium. Caesar, having settled matters in Thessaly, came to Alexandria, and when Pompeius's head and ring were brought to him and he saw them, he wept; and when he had withdrawn into the palace, he was cheated by the guardians, so that he should not receive money, as they cunningly stripped their own temples, both to show that the royal treasures were empty and to stir the people to ill will against Caesar. Besides, Achillas, the royal general, once steeped in Pompeius's blood, was planning Caesar's death too. For, ordered to dismiss the army he commanded, of 20,000 armed men, he not only scorned the order but also drew up a line of battle. In that battle the royal fleet, which happened to be drawn up on shore, was ordered to be burned. When the flame had invaded part of the city too, it burned up 400,000 books stored, as it happened, in the nearest buildings, a singular monument indeed of the study and care of the forefathers, who had gathered so many and such great works of distinguished minds. So, although even today there stand in the temples, as we ourselves have seen, chests for books, which, when they were plundered, were emptied by men of ours in our own times, as they relate (which indeed is true), yet it is more honourably believed that other books were sought to rival the former care for studies, than that there was then any other library which could be believed to have been outside the 400,000 books and so to have escaped. Caesar afterwards took the island where Pharos is. Achillas came there with the Gabinian soldiers. A huge battle was joined: a great multitude of Caesar's soldiers fell there, and all the killers of Pompeius were killed too. Caesar, pressed by the force of the enemy bearing down, got into a skiff: when it was soon weighed down and sunk by the weight of those following him, he reached a ship two hundred paces off by swimming, with one hand raised, in which he held his papers; soon, attacked in a sea battle, with great good fortune he either sank or took the royal fleet.

## 6.16

At the request of the Alexandrians he gave back their king, warning him to be eager to try Roman friendship rather than Roman arms; yet as soon as he was free he made war, but at once he himself was destroyed with his whole army. For 20,000 men are said to have been cut down in that war, 12,000 surrendered with seventy warships, and five hundred of the victors are said to have fallen. The young king himself, taken into a skiff to flee, was sunk and drowned as many leapt in; his body, rolled to the shore, was recognised by the sign of his golden breastplate: Caesar sent it ahead to Alexandria and forced all the Alexandrians by despair to surrender, and gave the kingdom of Egypt to Cleopatra. From there he went through Syria and defeated Pharnaces in Pontus. After he came to Rome, he was made dictator and consul, crossed into Africa, fought at Thapsus with Juba and Scipio, and killed a very great multitude of men there. The camps of both were plundered and sixty elephants taken. Cato killed himself at Utica; Juba gave a price to a man to strike him and offered him his throat; Petreius ran himself through with the same sword; Scipio, in the ship in which he was trying to flee to Spain and had been forced back to Africa by the wind, cut his own throat. In the same ship T. Torquatus too was killed. Caesar ordered the grandsons of Pompeius the Great and his daughter Pompeia to be killed, and with them Faustus Sulla, Afranius and the son of Petreius. Then, having entered the city with four triumphs and set in order the state of the recovered commonwealth, he at once set out for the Spains against the Pompeii, the sons of Pompeius, reached Saguntum on the seventeenth day after he had left the city, and at once waged many battles, with varying fortune, against the two Pompeii and Labienus and Attius Varus. The last battle was fought at the river Munda, where they fought with such strength and such slaughter was done that Caesar too, when even his veterans were not ashamed to give way and he saw his line being cut down and driven in, thought of forestalling by death the disgrace of being conquered, when suddenly the army of the Pompeii turned to flight and gave way. Indeed this battle was fought on the same day on which Pompeius the father had fled from the city to wage war, and for four years this civil war thundered without ceasing over the whole world. T. Labienus and Attius Varus were cut down in the line; Gnaeus Pompeius fled with a hundred horsemen. His brother Sextus Pompeius, having quickly gathered no small band of Lusitanians, joined battle with Caesonius, was defeated, and was killed as he fled. The city of Munda was barely taken, with immense slaughter of men, as Caesar stormed it.

## 6.17

Caesar returned to Rome. There, while he was mercifully restoring the state of the commonwealth, against the examples of the forefathers, at the instigation of Brutus and Cassius, and with very many of the senate privy to it, he was stabbed with twenty-three wounds in the senate house and died. They say there were more than sixty privy to that conspiracy. The two Bruti and C. Cassius and the other partners withdrew to the Capitol with daggers drawn. It was long debated whether the Capitol should be burned together with the authors of the murder. The people, goaded by grief, snatched up his body and burned it in the forum with fragments of the platforms and benches. Rome reviewed the breadth of her realm by her own disasters, and, turned to slaughter of herself, avenged each separate people in the very place where she had tamed it. From Asia, Europe and Africa, I do not say from three parts of the world but from all the corners of the three parts, she put out her gladiators and thrust on her enemies, taking holiday, the show of a wretched vengeance. Nor yet is it enough that the causes themselves too were consumed together with their authors; seeds that come up again sprout in the same field, bound to produce at once, with great sweat, great increases of evils for those who reap: Caesar, victor in the civil war, is killed by citizens; whole columns of men privy to it are drawn into the killing of one. For it was certain that Caesar, killed unworthily, could have many avengers; and very many of the nobility are coupled together in one chain of crime, so that so great a store of evils should not perhaps be thinned by the brevity of vengeance instead of being made good by the greatness of a war. The fables say that Medea once sowed the teeth of a slain serpent, from which, as a crop fitting the seed, armed men rose out of the earth and soon laid each other low by fighting. This indeed the inventions of the poets made up; but our Rome, when Caesar was killed, what armed columns she brought forth from his ashes! What wars she stirred up in witness of a wretched fruitfulness, not to be read by boys but to be watched by peoples! And yet the beginning of all these evils is pride: from it the civil wars blazed up, from it they sprouted again. So the slaughter of those who unjustly pursue slaughter is not unjust, if the rivalry of ambition is both carried on and punished through themselves and in themselves, until those who refused partnership learn to bear lordship, and, the sum of the whole empire being brought back to one, all men take up a far different kind of living, so that all are eager to please humbly, not to give offence insolently. But for so healthy a teaching of humility a master is needed. And so, when the affairs of Augustus Caesar had been fittingly settled, the Lord Christ was born, who, though He was in the form of God, humbly took on the form of a servant, so that the teaching of humility might at last be more fitting, when the punishment of pride was now an example to all throughout the whole world.

## 6.18

In the 710th year from the founding of the city, when Julius Caesar had been killed, Octavian, who by the will of Julius Caesar, his great-uncle, had taken on both the inheritance and the name, and who afterwards, when he gained supreme power, was called Augustus, as soon as he came to Rome, quite a young man, vowed his talents to civil wars. For, to set out briefly the heaping up of evils, he waged five civil wars: of Mutina, Philippi, Perusia, Sicily and Actium. Of these he fought two, that is the first and the last, against M. Antonius, the second against Brutus and Cassius, the third against L. Antonius, the fourth against Sex. Pompeius, son of Cn. Pompeius. Antonius, declared an enemy by the senate, had shut in D. Brutus by siege at Mutina. The consuls Hirtius and Pansa, and Caesar with them, were sent to free Brutus and defeat Antonius. Pansa, coming first, was caught in an ambush, and amid the slaughter of his men he himself too was heavily wounded by a javelin and died of the same wound some days later. Hirtius, bringing help to his colleague, destroyed great forces of Antonius with vast slaughter. Caesar meanwhile guarded the camp. In the second battle against Antonius great slaughter was done on both sides. For then and there the consul Hirtius was killed, Antonius fled defeated, and Caesar gained the victory; D. Brutus confessed to him his part in the conspiracy to kill Julius Caesar and poured out prayers of repentance. Dolabella killed at Smyrna Trebonius, one of Caesar's killers. The senate declared Dolabella an enemy. The armies of both slain consuls obeyed Caesar. Afterwards D. Brutus was taken and killed in Gaul by the Sequani. Basilus, likewise one of the assassins, was put to death by a band of his own slaves. With Lepidus busy about it, Caesar received Antonius back into favour, and as a pledge of the reconciled favour he took his daughter in marriage. Then, when they had come to the city and a rumour of a coming proscription had arisen, C. Thoranius, a man of praetorian rank, fearing nothing of the kind, was killed in his own house in an attack by soldiers, and very many others were butchered. And so, so that the slaughter should not be carried on more widely and more unbridled without limit, the names of 132 senators were put up on a list, first by the order and in the name of Lepidus, then of Antonius, third of Caesar. There Antonius had proscribed Tullius Cicero, his enemy, and there his own uncle L. Caesar, and, what was added to heighten the crime, while his mother was alive; there Lepidus had thrown his own brother L. Paulus into the same herd of the proscribed. Thirty Roman knights were afterwards added to the number of the proscribed. Many and various slaughters were carried on for a long time, and the houses of the proscribed were plundered of everything and pulled down. But Dolabella waged many battles in Syria with Cassius, and, defeated by him, killed himself. Brutus and Cassius, having got together great armies, met at Athens and laid waste all Greece. Cassius attacked the Rhodians by land and sea and forced them to surrender, and left them nothing but their lives. So Caesar and Antonius pursued them into Macedonia with great preparations for war and drove them to their deaths; although that battle was then most plainly won not by the valour of Antonius's side but by the good fortune of Caesar. For Caesar was then sick, and when he had resolved to keep himself in the camp to take rest, at the urging and prayers of his physician, who declared that he had been warned in a dream to lead Caesar out of the camp that day for the sake of his safety, he went out with difficulty onto the field among the forces, and soon his camp was taken by the enemy. But Caesar's soldiers in turn took the camp of Cassius. So Brutus and Cassius, driven to despair, brought an untimely death on themselves before the end of the war. For they called in men to strike them, and Cassius offered his head, Brutus his side. But at Rome Fulvia, wife of Antonius and mother-in-law of Caesar, was wielding lordship as a woman, so that it is uncertain, in this change of consular and royal height, whether she should be counted the last of a failing power or the first of a beginning one; certainly she was insolent even towards those through whom she was able to be insolent. For she attacked Caesar too on his return at Brundisium with insults, factions and plots. Driven off by him, she withdrew to Antonius in Greece. Sex. Pompeius, after he learned that he had been put on the list of the proscribed, turned to brigandage and laid waste the whole coast of Italy with slaughter and plunder. Having seized Sicily and blocked the supplies, he afflicted Rome with hunger. Soon the triumvirs, not to say tyrants, that is Lepidus, Caesar and Antonius, made peace with him. But at once, when Pompeius, against the agreement, enrolled runaways, he was held as an enemy. Mena, Pompeius's freedman, deserted to Caesar with a fleet of sixty ships, and by Caesar's order he himself commanded the same fleet. And the same man at once, with Statilius Taurus, waged a sea war against Menecrates, Pompeius's admiral. Then Caesar himself fought a most bloody sea war against the same Pompeians; but at once he lost almost his whole victorious fleet in a shipwreck at Scylaceum. Ventidius routed in three very great battles the Persians and Parthians bursting into Syria, and killed their king Pacorus in the line, on the very same day, that is, on which Crassus had been killed by the Parthians. Antonius, having barely stormed one fort, made peace with Antiochus, so that he himself might seem to have finished so great an affair. He put Ventidius in charge of Syria and ordered him to make war on Antigonus, who had then happened to conquer the Jews, take Jerusalem, plunder the temple, and give the kingdom to Herod: Ventidius at once defeated him and received him in surrender. Mena the freedman went back to Pompeius with six ships; mercifully received by him, he burned Caesar's fleet, although Caesar had lately lost another in a second shipwreck. The same Mena afterwards, surrounded by Agrippa in a sea battle, went over to Caesar with six triremes. But Caesar, granting this man, a deserter for the third time, only his life, left him idle. Then Agrippa fought a sea battle between Mylae and Lipara against Demochares and Pompeius and won, and there he then either sank or took thirty ships, the rest being battered. Pompeius fled to Messana. Caesar meanwhile had crossed to Tauromenium: Pompeius struck him with a sudden attack: from which, many of his ships sunk and a great multitude of his soldiers lost, Caesar fled into Italy; and without any delay he returned to Sicily; and there he met Lepidus coming from Africa, claiming the chief share for himself with terror, threats and arrogance. A few days later Agrippa, by Caesar's order, as Caesar watched from the shore with his line drawn up, clashed with Pompeius in a most fierce sea battle and won. For he either sank or took 163 ships. Pompeius barely slipped away with seventeen ships. Lepidus, swelling with great insolence from his twenty legions, when he had plundered Messana, given over to his soldiers, scorned Caesar himself once and again as he came to him, and rather ordered him to be attacked with missiles. Caesar avoided them by fending them off with his cloak gathered on his left arm; soon, spurring his horse, he returned to his own men and came against Lepidus with his army drawn up; and with only a few killed he forced very many of Lepidus's legions to cross over to his side. Lepidus, understanding at last where his vanity was tending, laid aside his general's cloak and put on dark clothing, became a suppliant to Caesar, and obtained his life and goods, though banished in perpetual exile. Taurus, Caesar's prefect, received into allegiance almost the whole of Sicily, tested by the sword and terrified. Forty-four legions were then under the command of Caesar alone; the soldiers, made fiercer by their numbers, stirred up some uproars to receive lands; but Caesar, great in spirit, discharged 20,000 soldiers, restored 30,000 slaves to their masters, and crucified 6,000 whose masters did not appear. He entered the city in an ovation, and it was decreed by the senate that he should hold the tribunician power for ever. In these days, across the Tiber, a spring of oil welled out of the ground from an inn for hire and flowed the whole day in a most ample stream.

## 6.19

Antonius, after he crossed the Araxes, surrounded by all evils on every side, at last barely got back to Antioch with a few men. For not only did he always flee defeated, by the multitude of horsemen and arrows, from all the battles, of which he tried several, but besides, hampered by the uncertain and unknown places of the region, he was forced by the heaviest famine to unspeakable food; very many of his soldiers surrendered to the enemy. From there he crossed into Greece and ordered Pompeius, who after his defeat by Caesar was restoring his army and the war, to come to him with a few men. Pompeius, fleeing, was often defeated in war by land and sea by Titius and Furnius, generals of Antonius, and was taken and a little after killed.
